(2¼ <em>pp. Received by the Dolphin in July</em>, 1647.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Proud</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-028" href="#sec-05-fn-028" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-028" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> <span class="sc">and Samuel Brown, aboard the</span> <em>ANTELOPE</em> [<span class="sc">at Swally</span>], <span class="sc">to the Company, January</span> 26, 1647 (<em>O.C.</em> 2026).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Narrate their voyage from England.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-029" href="#sec-05-fn-029" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-029" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> After passing the Cape they decided to save time by going outside Madagascar and refreshing at Mauritius; but their intention was frustrated by contrary winds and, having many of the crew down with scurvy, they were forced to put into St. Augustine’s Bay. Found some difficulty in procuring cattle, ‘not haveing the current trade, which is an Indian bead called vaca’; but they managed to get a few in exchange for brass wire and ‘arangoes<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-030" href="#sec-05-fn-030" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-030" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup>’. Sailing on September 20 [<em>sic</em>] they on October 8 made the African coast to the northward of Mozambique. Currents and want of wind hindered their progress, with the result that, on sighting Mayotta, they deemed it prudent to put in there. After refitting their ship, they sailed on November 10, sighted the Arabian coast on December 20, and arrived here January 24. Complain of the beer supplied to the ships, twenty of the butts having had to be thrown overboard. The salt beef was of poor quality and cut into too small pieces. That sent for a trial, ‘being pickled with a smaller [i.e. weaker] sort of pickle’, was found to keep quite as well as the rest. (<em>Copy. 2¾ pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-136"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="136" data-folio="98" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n135_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 98">[p. 98]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Philip Wylde and Francis Scattergood at Achin to the President and Council at Surat, January</span> 28, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">A</span>, p. 112).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote last by the <em>Seahorse</em> on October 30, but cannot prepare a transcript before the <em>Friendship</em> sails. The markets here are very dull. Great quantities of pepper and tin are expected this year, and they will endeavour to buy some as soon as they have sold their cloth. (<em>Copy. Original received March</em> 31.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Winter and Richard Hudson at Masulipatam to the President and Council at Surat, January</span> 29, 1647 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 90).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forward some letters received from Madraspatam for transmission to Surat. Wonder that the Agent should complain of their remissness in relieving that place, as this is quite untrue. ‘Sure the feare of warr and fammine hath amazed him, that he knows not what he writs.’ They have already ‘largly releived him’, and yesterday the <em>Seaflower</em>, having finished trimming, was dispatched to him with the merchandise and provisions noted in the enclosed invoice. Henry Olton and John Brown have gone in her, to take passage for Bantam. Last year a junk called the ‘<em>Derry Dowlett</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-031" href="#sec-05-fn-031" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-031" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’ sailed from this place for Mokha, piloted by one John Gayton. She arrived there in safety and left again last August; but nothing has been heard of her since, and it is feared that some pirate may have taken her. Would be glad of any intelligence on the subject. The Mīr Jumla has dispatched two junks from this place to Gombroon and Mokha respectively. On the former are certain goods belonging to him, and the enclosed copies of letters of advice to the chief factor will show what he desires. ‘We doe earnestly intreat you to grant his request; otherwise he will not suffer us any quietness in this place.’ The junk for Mokha is piloted by Richard Walwyn, who has behaved very civilly while here. Mīr Jumla is about to send another junk to Pegu, laden with his own and freight goods. On the 16th present Courteen’s <em>Rebecca</em>, with Thomas Clark as cape merchant, arrived here from Bengal. She was fully freighted with Moors’ goods, part for this place and part for Persia. Clark hoped <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-137" data-page="137" data-folio="99" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n136_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 99">[p. 99]</button> to procure further freight here; but on the factors warning the Governor that the vessel was none of the Company’s and that they could not be in any way responsible for goods put into her, he discouraged the merchants from embarking anything. Fearing, however, that she might surprise some of Mīr Jumla’s vessels at sea, he spoke Clark fairly, lent him a hundred pagodas for expenses, and promised to provide a house for a factory on his return next May or June. Hardly believe the vessel will come back, for ‘Clarke hath not forgott his lyinge straine’, and her crew are deserting so fast at every opportunity that she has but eighteen on board. <em>PS.</em> —Forbear to send a transcript of their letter of October 14 last, as they understand that the original has reached Surat. <em>PPS.</em>—The <em>Falcon’s</em> ledger goes herewith. (<em>Copy. Original received March</em> 12.)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">George Tash, Hugh Fenn, and Anthony Smith at Ahmadābād to the President and Council at Surat</span>, <span class="sc">February</span> 2, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">a</span>, p. 48).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Advise having drawn bills on Surat ‘to supply our severall factories’. Are much troubled by ‘the diers dishonnest dealing with us; who, in this tyme of multiplycity of bussiness, send in our goods cured in such evill manner as that wee cannot receive them without assurance of after complaints against us; and when wee returne them to bee perfected, wee send them to lye by the walles, since with others (they say) such ill dyed goods will finde acceptance, and such men must then bee first served.’ Cannot promise, therefore, to have the goods ready before the 20th, though they are doing their best to hasten them. Provision of saltpetre at ‘Malpore’. The ‘cannekeens’ and ‘ardeas’ provided in ‘Sunganier<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-032" href="#sec-05-fn-032" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-032" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’ have been examined, and the factors find ‘there curing, both in the dyinge and beating, farr to exceed any that is performed in this place; there price being alsoe more reasonable.’ Request early instructions regarding the piece-goods wanted for Bantam. Owing to the recent drought, the water in the river is low, and the longer the work is deferred the more expensive it will prove. ‘Our store of Sunganier mercoles will be sufficient to supply a large quantity of tappichindaes and chints broad.’ Are anxious to receive a fitting present <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-138" data-page="138" data-folio="100" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n137_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 100">[p. 100]</button> for the Governor, as without it they cannot visit him or thank him for the favours granted to them. Express their gratitude for the wine sent up. Money received from Mīr Mūsā by Tash on behalf of Alī Akbar. Nothing having been heard of the ‘cossetts’ [<em>qāsid</em>, a letter-carrier] from Agra, it is feared that they have miscarried. Have drawn further bills on Surat, and would be glad to be furnished with money up to 80,000 rupees. (<em>Copy. Original received February</em> 8.)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Philip Wylde and Francis Scattergood at Achin to the President and Council at Surat, February</span> 3, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">a</span>, p. 111).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">[<em>First part missing.</em>] Request the early supply of three house servants, ‘honest, if possible.’ Proposed purchase of camphor. Courteen’s <em>Friendship</em> brought some Portuguese goods from Malacca, and is now bound for Goa and thence to Rājāpur.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-033" href="#sec-05-fn-033" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-033" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Captain John [Smart] is very sick and is not likely to recover. [Scattergood?] desires an increase of salary. <em>PS.—</em>‘Captain John Smart at sealing heerof departed this life, and William C[o]ck, a young youth, succeeds him in place.’ (<em>Copy. Original received March</em> 31.)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Robert Heynes and Thomas Best at Gombroon to the President and Council at Surat, February</span> 5, 1647<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-034" href="#sec-05-fn-034" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-034" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> (<em>O.C.</em> 2027).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote last by the Dutch <em>Delftshaven</em>, which sailed on January 25. Enclose a list of sales at Ispahān. The Sind goods are not in much demand in that place, and any future consignments will be disposed of here. Of the ‘Matchawarra baftaes’, the finer qualities yield considerable profit, but the poor sorts are unvendible. The same applies to ‘hummumees and sallooes’. The ‘cocheaw cloth<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-035" href="#sec-05-fn-035" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-035" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>’ is much sought after, and they would be glad of a large supply of the best quality. Enumerate the cotton goods sold here,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-036" href="#sec-05-fn-036" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-036" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-139" data-page="139" data-folio="101" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n138_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 101">[p. 101]</button> and the prices realized. The profit made may appear small, because they have rated the pagoda at 32 shāhīs (in accordance with Wylde’s books), whereas it is only worth two rials of eight, which are reckoned at 14 shāhīs apiece. Having so much pepper on hand, and more expected, they have sold some at 16½ lārīs per Surat maund of 33 lb.—‘a most despicable price’. Are now seeking freight for the <em>Falcon</em>, and hope to dispatch her to Surat by the 10th instant. ‘The Dutch shipp <em>Lewark</em>, which set saile from Swally Hole the night after ours, arrived here the first currant with Sr. Vanbourrough and Sr. Bastian,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-037" href="#sec-05-fn-037" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-037" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> whoe are nowe prepareing for their journey to court; but if it bee true (as it is reported) that the King goes after Xoorooze<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-038" href="#sec-05-fn-038" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-038" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> in person against the Indian, whoe hath prowdly challenged him the feild, hee may runn the wildgoose chase before he overtakes him.’ Neither of the expected English ships has yet appeared. (<em>Copy.</em> 2 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">George Tash at Ahmadābād to the President and Council at Surat, February</span> 7, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii A, p. 52).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Did not receive the Surat letters till three days ago. Among them he found one requiring the instant dispatch of the goods for Mokha. Encloses details of those which he hopes to forward shortly. (<em>Copy.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">John Spiller, Henry Garry, Nicholas Scrivener, and Gilbert Harrison [at Tatta] to the President and Council at Surat, [February</span> 11<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-039" href="#sec-05-fn-039" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-039" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>, 1647] (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 75).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Are still detained here by the scarcity of new rupees. ‘Heere is but one day in a weeke that rupees are stampt’, and most of them fall into the hands of the Dīwān. They tried to get a supply of last year’s rupees instead, but found that these also were very scarce. However, the Kandiāro investment is not being much <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-140" data-page="140" data-folio="102" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n139_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 102">[p. 102]</button> delayed, for they have managed to remit 1,000 rupees by exchange to the broker there, and this will find him employment until they arrive. The Shāhbandar is urging the merchants to ship their goods, especially to Mokha, but hitherto with scant success. ‘Bumbah’ [Bāmbā], the chief trader in that direction, died about three months ago, and his estate was thereupon seized; and most of the others are disinclined to send any goods. The Shāhbandar has informed the Dīwān and Bakhshī ‘that he will not meddle now with Bunder, unless each of them send a man to sitt in custome house with one that he shall apointe for his part. Soe heere merchants have very ill quarter.’ Dealings with the Shāhbandar about their broadcloth. He is about to return to Lārībandar. Supply of funds to Kandiāro and Nasarpur. (<em>Copy.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">George Tash, Hugh Fenn, and Anthony Smith at Ahmadābād to the President and Council at Surat, February</span> 12, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">a</span>, p. 61).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Complain of the tardy delivery of the letters and bills of exchange from Surat, and opine that the shroffs may have bribed the messenger. ‘The slacknes of our Byram<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-040" href="#sec-05-fn-040" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-040" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> men in bringing in their cloth’ made it doubtful whether the desired quantity could be procured; but, by giving them some small encouragement, 100 corge have been obtained and are now ready to accompany the goods intended for Basra. No ‘mercooles’ or ‘eckbarrees’ are at present available. Have already advised as to saltpetre. The ‘Sunganeer’ goods will be sent down with the rest, except the baftas, which are so poor that they will be returned to the buyer. Complain that they have not been furnished with a horse, or permitted to buy one here. Rejoice at the arrival of the <em>Antelope</em>. Have received the bills of exchange for 50,000 rupees, and trust they will be duly met; ‘whereof the dayly failing of the sheroffs and merchants here putts us into some jealousye.’ Deny the charges of harsh dealing made against them by the shroffs, and express surprise that these should be listened to at Surat. Have duly forwarded the letters, &amp;c., addressed to Agra. Will provide ‘sahabees<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-041" href="#sec-05-fn-041" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-041" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>’ for Achin; also the ‘atlas<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-042" href="#sec-05-fn-042" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-042" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>’ desired, but cannot <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-141" data-page="141" data-folio="103" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n140_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 103">[p. 103]</button> get any of the required dimensions. Of ‘Finckun<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-043" href="#sec-05-fn-043" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-043" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’ there are four or five hundred maunds in town; the ordinary sort costs seven or eight rupees per maund and the better, called Chowkee [<em>chokhā</em>, good, or fine], ten or eleven. On receipt of the remittances from Surat, they called in some bills already given; would be glad of 20,000 or 30,000 rupees more. Send on ‘musters’ received from Agra. Goods for Mokha and Suakin were dispatched in a caravan on the 8th. Payments to be made to the conductor, &amp;c. The Basra investment goes on slowly, ‘and our dyers continue to abuse us in couleringe our goods most intollerably and beyond all sufferance.’ Mīrzā Alī Akbar should be reminded to send up his bill of debt to be cleared here. <em>P.S.</em>—Having an opportunity of furnishing the Tatta factory with 5,000 rupees, they are taking bills at two per cent. profit, less half a rupee [per 100?] for insurance. In order to pay two bills, amounting to 12,000 rupees, on account of the Agra merchants, they have drawn on Surat for that sum at 1½ per cent. loss. Forward copies of their last year’s accounts. (<em>Copy. Original received February</em> 18.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">John Lewis at Gombroon to the President and Council at Surat, February</span> 12, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">a</span>, p. 83).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Account of his voyage from Swally. The <em>Francis</em>, under Rickman, parted company for Basra on January 13. Gombroon was reached on February 6. In the road they found a small junk and a Dutch ship. The other Hollander, which left Swally a day after they did and had ‘the new commandore’ on board, was met with off Ormus. The <em>Seahorse</em>, which had lost company on January 28, arrived on February 9. (<em>Copy. Original received March</em> 12.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Heynes, John Lewis, and Thomas Best at Gombroon to the President and Council at Surat, February</span> 13, 1647 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 78).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Now reply to the letters received by the <em>Endeavour</em> and <em>Seahorse</em>. As the advices sent by way of Sind were so long detained, they will abstain from using that route in future. The goods that were forwarded to Ispahān were thus disposed of in the hope they would find better markets there; but the event has proved otherwise, ‘by reason <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-142" data-page="142" data-folio="104" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n141_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 104">[p. 104]</button> of the finall discourse<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-044" href="#sec-05-fn-044" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-044" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> of merchants cominge to that place, occasioned through the warrs twixt this King and the Turke, which caused the way of Bagdatt to bee stopt.’ Have already advised what has been sold since the departure of Pitt. Will do their best to obtain for the Company its rightful share of the customs; but if the Shāhbandar goes on as he has begun, they are not likely to receive even as much as last year, ‘for a more notorious theife lives not’. Since they are ordered to be content with what they can get by fair means, they will obey; but ‘when the Company are forced to seeke theire due, these base people will finde that forbearance will be noe acquitance’. Will not contradict the statement that the Dutch have obtained a farmān for silk, ‘since you have beene informed that they have one’. Will remember to procure gammons of bacon, neats’ tongues, &amp;c., against next year; such things are not to be had here, but they will endeavour to obtain them ‘at our repaire to Spahaun’. Have not finished unlading the two pinnaces, and so cannot certify the correctness of their invoices; but they learn that only half the number of pepper bags can be found, and this will much delay the work. Hear nothing of any tin sent hither by the Dutch. Have sold most of their own at 50 lārīs the maund of 33 lb., and expect to dispose of the rest ‘suddainely’. Lewis has safely arrived, and will take over the accounts from Heynes. Particulars of the <em>Falcon’s</em> cargo from this place. Pearls (?) sent for delivery to ‘Chout<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-045" href="#sec-05-fn-045" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-045" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>’, who is to pay for them at the rate of 30 rupees per tūmān, ‘the price current in this place at present’. Trumball has also received a small bag of ‘chickeens’ [sequins] for the Company’s account. The Dutch chief has kindly furnished the <em>Falcon</em> with the provisions mentioned in the enclosed note; request that recompense may be duly made to the Dutch ‘comodore’ at Surat. Money advanced to the ship’s purser. Courteen’s ship, the <em>Ruth</em>, came in on the same day as the <em>Seahorse</em>. Thompson, her commander, is very sick. Her lading is chiefly pepper, cotton wool, gumlac, and rice. The present price of pepper is only 15 lārīs per maund; and, if no more be offered, the <em>Ruth</em> will probably carry hers to England. The two pinnaces will be unladen and returned <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-143" data-page="143" data-folio="105" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n142_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 105">[p. 105]</button> to Surat as speedily as possible. <em>PS.</em>—Could not send any horses, because, on mentioning the matter to the Sultān, he declared that he had received orders from the King not to permit the shipment of any. They produced the royal farmān, allowing them to export twelve horses yearly, but this had no effect. However, they intend to petition the Itimād-uddaula on the subject, and doubt not to be able to send some shortly. Have invoiced back again the gunpowder that came from Masulipatam. Passage granted to an Italian padre. Money forwarded for delivery to Vīrjī Vōra. (<em>Copy. Original received March</em> 11.)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Robert Heynes, aboard the</span> <em>Falcon</em> <span class="sc">at Gombroon, to the President and Council at Surat, February</span> 13, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">a</span>, p. 83).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Advises that a bag of money has been brought on board at the last moment. It is to be delivered to a merchant in Surat, and the freight has been paid here. (<em>Copy. Original received March</em> 11.)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">President Breton and Thomas Merry at Swally Marine to the Company, February</span> 26, 1647 (<em>O. C.</em> 2028).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-046" href="#sec-05-fn-046" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-046" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote last by the <em>Dolphin</em>, which sailed on January 26. Their coral remains unsold, owing chiefly to the large proportion of the coarser kind. Most of the lead has been landed, as the Governor has promised to take it at last year’s rate. He also wishes to buy their broadcloth for the King, but nothing is yet settled. Are busy preparing cargoes for the next shipping. Knipe writes from Agra that he has already bought upwards of 400 bales of excellent indigo and hope to make the number up to 500. At Ahmadābād that commodity continues dear, and purchases will consequently be deferred. On February 12 arrived the Dutch <em>Delftshaven</em> from Gombroon, bringing 500 tūmāns from the English factors, and letters from Andrew Trumball, announcing that the <em>Falcon</em> had reached that port on January 21. Refer to his account of the sufferings of the crew for want of water [<em>see</em> p. 75]. Such difficulties are always to be expected, as the ‘small tradeing shipps’ are poorly <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-144" data-page="144" data-folio="106" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n143_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 106">[p. 106]</button> supplied with casks. Request a supply of pewter box compasses, lanterns, and half-hour and half-minute glasses. Sales in Persia. The Dutch <em>Leeuwerik</em> on February 20 brought a further letter from Gombroon, announcing that the <em>Falcon</em> was to sail five days after her. Intend to have the stock of pepper brought back hither, as it can only be sold at a loss in Persia. Have been unable until now to obtain freight for the <em>Antelope</em> for Gombroon, owing to Dutch competition; but have at last procured a sufficient quantity, though at low rates, and she will sail to-morrow. From Gombroon she will go to Madraspatam, carrying 15,000 rials of eight, part of the 25,000 ordered to be invested there and the proceeds sent hither by next November. Owing to the delay in the arrival of the <em>Falcon</em> (now hourly expected), the idea of sending her to Suakin has been abandoned and she will only go to Mokha, proceeding thence in July to Tuticorin. She will there seek a supply of ‘cattches . . . a sort of cloth vendable in Persia to very good advance’; and will then ‘rainge the coast and try if pepper may bee procured att Coylon or Pourcatt and cynamon at Cocheen and those adjacent places.’ For these purposes she will be supplied with 15,000 rials of eight at Mokha. The <em>Lanneret</em> is to be sent to Suakin, and is now at Goghā to fetch that part of her lading which has been bought at Cambay, as this course will save six or seven per cent. compared with the expense of bringing the goods by land. Hope to dispatch her on her voyage in four or five days. In her place, the <em>Endeavour</em> (now expected from Gombroon) will be sent to the Coast and Pegu, carrying to the former place a supply of wheat and rice. The <em>Seahorse</em> is destined for Basra and the <em>Francis</em> for Bantam. The <em>Supply</em> is now voyaging to Rājāpur, to fetch a quantity of goods bought at Rāybāg; on her return she will be sent to Achin and Johore. ‘Upon the 22th current here arived the Duch shipp <em>Snuke</em> from Tywan, which is said to have brought sugar, copper, tuttanagle, and brimstone, but noe plate [i.e. silver]. Their trade there and in Japon (as wee collect from the Commandore) is much declined; the former by reason of warrs in Chyna which, begining first amongst themselves, gave oppertunity unto the Tarters to enter, who (as the Dutch affirme) have wholy conquered that rich and spacious contry, some porte townes excepted. The Dutch themselves also acknowledge that their Manella fleete, consisting of seven sayle, hath been disgracefully <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-145" data-page="145" data-folio="107" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n144_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 107">[p. 107]</button> beaten by two Spanish gallions, which were set out of Maneilles purposely to encounter them; wherof they sunk one, burnt another, enforced the rest to fly for safetie. This wee heere from their Commandore; but other of there people relate that there were eleven shipps, and that more of them were burnt.’ However, the Dutch are in no want of shipping, to judge by the number in these parts. Yesterday their flute <em>Juffrouw</em> set sail for Mokha, and with her the <em>Delftshaven</em> for Gombroon with freight goods, not above one-third laden. The <em>Haan</em> is also bound thither, but is not likely to be more than half full; and the <em>Snoek</em> will follow with sugar brought from Taiwan and some freight goods, if procurable. The <em>Leeuwerik</em> is to go to Mokha. The <em>Bergenopzoom</em> remains here empty, while the <em>Nassau</em> is expected from Persia. ‘The distracted story of the Courtinians you will receive from themselves, the <em>Sun</em> being bound home, by which wee hope these will bee safely conveighed unto you (though not entrusted unto any of them). Agent Farren and his Councell lately profferred us their fort att Carware; but, in regard it would have been a certaine charge unto you and happily might not have proved proporcionably advantagious, wee thought not fitting to accept therof; nor indeed could, without unmaning some of your shipps to keepe itt, which wee are confident would have been noe whitt pleasing unto you. Their shipp <em>James</em> they have sould att Goa, with guns &amp;c. appertainances, for 7,000 zeraphins, and reparted the money amongst them; of whose men wee have entertained four, vizt. Robert Woodruf, cheif carpenter, at 28<em>s.</em> per moneth, John Wood, boatswaine, att 25<em>s.</em> per moneth, and Phillip Hall and John Hatfeild, two able seamen, at 18<em>s.</em> The <em>Ruth</em> is gon for Persia, and will, wee hope, att her returne follow the <em>Sun</em> for England.’ The Portuguese are sending three ships to Europe this month. Enclose a letter received from Maximilian Bowman at Colombo, ‘where, itt seemes, hee is resolved to reside and (as wee heere) is suddainly to bee married, notwithstanding hee hath a wyfe and childe in England. Wee have earnestly sollicited the Vicroy to returne him unto us; but whether hee will or can grattifie us therein, the Inquisition haveing taken him into there protection, wee are doubtfull.’ Regret the loss of an able and diligent servant in Thomas Methwold, who died on February 11. <em>P.S.</em>—They are sending 10,000 rials to the Coast in the <em>Endeavour</em>, to be invested for Persia. (7 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-146"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="146" data-folio="108" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n145_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 108">[p. 108]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Cogan and William Weale at Basra to the President and Council at Surat, February</span> 27, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">A</span>, p. 103).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In reply to the letter brought by the <em>Francis</em>, which arrived on the 16th, they express their regret for omitting to write by the <em>Endeavour</em>. Will do their best to dispose of the goods, but at present they cannot find any customers. The pepper has been landed and agrees with the invoice. The messenger expected from Aleppo fell sick on the road, and they fear he is dead. Will inquire for any letters brought by him. Particulars of freight. (<em>Copy. Original received March</em> 26.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">George Tash, Hugh Fenn, and Anthony Smith at Ahmadābād to the President and Council at Surat, February</span> 28, 1647 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 72).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Finding it absolutely necessary to attend the Governor’s darbār, they, with the assistance of Mīr Mūsā, made up a present of red and green broadcloth, a mirror, and a set of barber’s instruments, and offered it for his acceptance. It was graciously received, and the Governor assured the factors of his goodwill towards them. Since then Tash has had several interviews with him, and has succeeded in clearing away all obstructions to their saltpetre business. Further, the Governor has not only granted a parwāna freeing the English from all way duties, but has authorized them to send out ‘a man of quallity’ to recover all such dues as have already been paid. Acquainted the Governor also of the 135 rupees ‘soe unjustly extracted from us at our first saltpeeters dismission’; but this, they found, had ‘fallen into his owne purse, and therefore the motion for restitucion was not soe well relished by him, who gave noe other reply thereto then that hee would enquire into the certainty thereof.’ Accordingly, they were dismissed with ‘the gift of two pammerins<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-047" href="#sec-05-fn-047" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-047" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> unto George Tash’. Present made also to the Bakhshī for his assistance. Request some broadcloth to repay Mīr Mūsā, with some ‘overplus’ as an acknowledgement of his many favours. Yesterday sent off another caravan. Payments to be made to the carters and guards. Have heard a rumour that <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-147" data-page="147" data-folio="109" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n146_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 109">[p. 109]</button> the Agra factors have sent a man to ‘Malpore’ to buy saltpetre; if this is true, they fear that competition will ensue, to the detriment of the Company. Prospects of the indigo crop. (<em>Copy. Original received March 6.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">David White</span>, aboard the <em>Lanneret</em> in Gog̲h̲ā Road, to the President at Surat, March 1, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">A</span>, p. 71).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Arrived here February 23. Nothing has been heard of the ‘caphila’; and, as the pilots say that, if it does not arrive by the 3rd, it cannot come until the next spring tide, he will after that date return to Swally. <em>PS.</em>—Denies the rumour that a boat has been sunk and a number of bales damaged. (<em>Copy.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">George Tash</span>, <span class="sc">Hugh Fenn</span>, and <span class="sc">Anthony Smith</span> at Ahmadābād to the President and Council at Surat, March 2, 1647 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 77).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">As already advised, ‘Commera Beage [Kamar Beg], a munsudar [mansabdār] of this place’, has been deputed to recover all way dues paid this year. Request that copies of the accounts (translated into Persian) may be sent to him at Broach, and that Abdul Karīm may be directed to attend him ‘to certifye the sayd disburstments.’ (<em>Copy. Original received March 6.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Robert Heynes</span>, <span class="sc">John Lewis</span>, and <span class="sc">Thomas Best</span> at Gombroon to the President and Council at Surat, March 4, 1647 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 92).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote last by the <em>Falcon</em>, which sailed on February 13. Were disappointed to receive no letters by the Dutch <em>Nassau</em>, which arrived on February 28. Quantities and prices of goods sold since the departure of the <em>Falcon</em>. The Madraspatam cloth is both poor and overpriced. The markets here are very bad and are likely to be worse, as a Dutch ship is daily expected from Masulipatam. There is no demand for the longcloth. The socalled ‘guldars<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-048" href="#sec-05-fn-048" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-048" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’ are found to be ‘but nimguls<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-049" href="#sec-05-fn-049" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-049" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, which are but halfe soe long as the guldars’; they are therefore more suitable <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-148" data-page="148" data-folio="110" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n147_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 110">[p. 110]</button> for Basra, and will be sent thither. The ‘cheleus<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-050" href="#sec-05-fn-050" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-050" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’ are so bad that no offer can be got for them, except one which involved a loss, while the brown ‘sallampores’ are too coarse to tempt buyers. The ‘Sindy’ goods have fetched but mean prices, as they are not so good as last year’s consignment and yet are high priced. The Sultān will not yet consent to the shipment of horses; but they have instructed Wilton to complain to the Itimād-uddaula on the subject, and meanwhile, if the <em>Francis</em> comes in, they will detain her passengers until leave is granted to put the horses aboard her. The Shāhbandar continues to defraud them in the matter of customs dues; only last night seven or eight boatloads of goods from the Dutch ship were carried to ‘the private custome house’. ‘The <em>Ruth</em> which we adviced of in our last, and as we thought belonged to Mr. Curtine, we have since learned is noe such matter, but that Alderman Andrews<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-051" href="#sec-05-fn-051" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-051" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> and Mr. Maurice Tompson are his cheife owners; who have obtained from the Esquire his pattent for licensing the shipp to come for India, allowing him three per cent. upon all goods shipt out, and his factors which are in India to asist them.’ Have received no answer to their protest, but this is ascribed to the sickness of her captain, who has not stirred out of doors since he came ashore. Part of her rice has been sold, and the rest of her cargo has been trucked for dates. She is now preparing to depart for Rājāpur, and is taking passengers for that port, to the detriment of the Company. Intend to dispatch part of their pepper to Ispahān for sale, and also to send some of the sandalwood to Basra; the rest might be returned to Surat, as it will not sell here. Deficiencies in the cargoes of the two pinnaces. Account of the money and rūnās now put on board them. Cannot hope for much freight. Request to be supplied with a great beam, as the one they have is very defective; also with butter, oil, rice, and candles, prices here being very high. Desire confirmation of their action in raising the salary of their linguist from 6 to 12 tūmāns per annum. <em>P.S.</em>—Correct an <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-149" data-page="149" data-folio="111" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n148_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 111">[p. 111]</button> error in the last invoice. Money advanced to the pursers of the two vessels. Amount received for freight. Two bags of money to be delivered to Mīrzā Mahmūd and Vīrjī Vōra. (<em>Copy. Original received March</em> 19.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Robert Heynes, John Lewis, and Thomas Best at Gombroon to the President and Council at Surat, March</span> 9, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">a</span>, p. 101).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>Francis</em> arrived from Basra yesterday, bringing intelligence that the markets there were very bad. The same may be said of this place. Pepper is in no demand at Ispahān; but Wilton has sold most of the other goods there and is about to send down the proceeds. If they cannot dispose of their stock here, they will be forced to forward it to Ispahān. Will detain the <em>Francis</em> till the limit of time allowed, and intend to keep all goods on board until permission is granted for the shipment of their horses. ‘The Dutch are mighty strang since the news of ther new Generall.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-052" href="#sec-05-fn-052" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-052" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Creditt us, we beleive they have some designe upon this place, which they will bring to effect by degrees, if the King condiscend not to there propositions; for the Comodore keepes the <em>Paw</em> in the road, pretending that for her oldness he dares not venture her [to] sea and, if she be not able to subsist this monzone, to runne her on shore.’ <em>PS.</em>—Hearing that sandalwood commands a good price at Basra, they have decided to send some thither in a hired vessel. (<em>Copy. Original received March</em> 26.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas [Rick]man, on board the</span> <em>Francis</em> <span class="sc">at Gombroon, to the President and Council at Surat, [March</span> 10?] 1647 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 105).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Left Basra on March 2, and arrived here on the 8th. Would have proceeded to Surat in company with the Dutch <em>Nassau</em>, but the factors have resolved to detain his vessel. (<em>Copy. Original received March</em> 26.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-150"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="150" data-folio="112" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n149_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 112">[p. 112]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">George Tash, Hugh Fenn, and Anthony Smith at Ahmadābād to the President and Council at Surat</span>, <span class="sc">March</span> 13, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">a</span>, p. 98).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Advise the dispatch on the 8th of the ‘sahebies’ and ‘atlas’ required for Achin. Heard lately from ‘Malpore’ that ‘our people’ had made an agreement for 1,500 great maunds of saltpetre at 22 rupees per maund, which is one-eighth dearer than last year, but this is due to the high cost of transport, owing to the lack of water and grass caused by the drought. In reply instructed them to double the quantity. Await instructions regarding the investment for Bantam. Suggest that they be authorized to provide 150 or 200 corge of baftas while they are cheap. <em>P.S.</em>—Details of the cost of the ‘sahebies’ and ‘atlas’. (<em>Copy. Original received March</em> 19.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Revett Walwyn at Broach to the President and Council at Surat, March</span> 20, 1647 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 100).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Cannot induce the dyers and beaters to promise more than 20 corge of cloth each day, but will endeavour to raise this to 24 or 25 corge. Requests instructions as to the marks to be put on the bales. (<em>Copy. Original received March</em> 22.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">George Tash, Hugh Fenn, and [Anthony] Smith at Ahmadābād to the President and Council at Surat</span>, [<span class="sc">March</span> 20?] 1647 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 105).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Received their letters on the 16th, and note the instructions regarding the dyeinghouse. Refining of saltpetre. Goods required for Bantam. ‘Rarieties’ desired by the Governor. Regret that what they wrote about a horse is resented at Surat; they never intended to pay more than 250 rupees for one. Complain also that the shroffs’ accusations against them should have been credited so readily. Have heard nothing yet as to the recovery of way dues. Have been vainly trying to reduce the rate of interest to <sup>3</sup>⁄<sub>4</sub> per cent. [per month?]. Advise having drawn some bills of exchange. [Joyce?] renews his application for an increase of salary. (<em>Copy.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-151"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="151" data-folio="113" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n150_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 113">[p. 113]</button></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Robert Heynes, John Lewis, and Thomas Best at Gombroon to the President and Council at Surat</span>, <span class="sc">March</span> 23, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">A</span>, p. 113).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote last by the <em>Nassau</em> on the 19th [<em>sic</em>]. Little has occurred since. Have sold part of their pepper, but at a poor price, due largely to Dutch competition. The Sultān has at last granted them leave to ship off horses, and two (besides two mules) are now sent. The Shāhbandar continues his robberies in the matter of the customs. Forward money, as specified in the invoice. Passages granted to certain men. Are surprised that no ships have arrived from India. <em>PS.</em>—Copies of the last accounts are sent herewith. Money advanced to the purser of the <em>Francis</em>. (<em>Copy. Original received April</em> 5.)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">President Breton and Thomas Merry at Swally Marine to the Company, March</span> 31, 1647 (<em>O.C.</em> 2031).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Enclose a transcript of their previous letter by the <em>Sun</em>, which was to leave Rājāpur about the middle of this month. The <em>Falcon</em>, after another tedious passage, arrived here March 11, and eleven days later was dispatched to Mokha. She is so ‘walt and ill condiciond’ that she needs two feet depth of stones to ballast her; this much limits her carrying capacity and consequently she is not a profitable ship. Intend on her return to consult the commanders and carpenters as to remedying her defects. Her voyage from Mokha to Tuticorin has been countermanded, owing to the illness of the broker there employed; and in lieu thereof, Oxenden has been instructed to proceed in her to Porakād, Cochin, &amp;c., to buy pepper and cinnamon. The voyage of the <em>Lanneret</em> to Suakin has been postponed to next year, owing to an accident; a boat bringing off the goods bought in Cambay was ‘sunke by that boare <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-053" href="#sec-05-fn-053" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-053" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’, and though the bales were recovered, ‘that designe became therby frustraite.’ She is now bound for Basra <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-054" href="#sec-05-fn-054" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-054" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, as the <em>Seahorse</em> was thought at Gombroon to be too leaky for that voyage. However, upon the arrival here of that vessel (with the <em>Endeavour</em>) on the 19th current, her leak was found to be stopped, and so they <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-152" data-page="152" data-folio="114" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n151_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 114">[p. 114]</button> dispatched her, nine days later, to Mokha. Learning, by letters from the Coast, ‘what a miserable condicion Madraspatam and that whole cuntry are declined unto through warr and famine, and how much your Agent &amp;c. suffer’, they have decided to send the <em>Francis</em> thither instead of to Bantam, hoping that ‘shee will not only doe them very good servis in supplyeing them with provissions, but may happily awe the Portuges, their neighbors, unto better abearances; betwixt whome you will finde there have beene some late differences, which also the Viceroy hath signified unto us, with some complaints of our people, very earnestly desiring they may bee reconsiled; which wee doubt not facilly to effect, and indeede wee sumthing wonder how it comes to passe they should continue so to differ, wee in these partes maintaning as faire a corrispondency with the Vicroy and that whole nacion, and receave as much respect from them, as wee can expect or desire’. Letters from the Basra factors, brought to Gombroon by the <em>Francis</em> and thence by the Dutch <em>Nassau</em> (which arrived here five days ago), complain exceedingly of bad and dead markets; while similar complaints come from Gombroon and Achin. Unless prospects improve at the last-named place, and ‘if Johore encourage us not’, they will probably ‘wholly desert those partes’. The chief cause of this decline of trade is the excessive export of Indian commodities, and the effect is seen in the scarcity and dearness of the latter, especially indigo. At Agra none can be obtained under 43 rupees the maund, while at Ahmadābād there has been no drop in the prices formerly advised. They are therefore investing chiefly in calicoes and saltpetre. The present Governor of Ahmadābād has given them permission to export the latter, and they also expect a good quantity from Agra. Their greatest want is now the <em>Greyhound</em>, which has not yet arrived. Cannot get rid of their coral. Several chests have been shipped in the <em>Falcon</em>, to be sold, if possible, on the Malabar Coast. The saltpetre detained at Rāybāg has now been released, on giving a bribe of 500 pagodas to Mustafa Khān, and its receipt is daily expected. They doubt whether it will be safe to make any further investment in those parts, because of the indebtedness of the ‘Courtinians’, amounting to 17,000 pagodas. Of this sum 5,400 pagodas are due to Mustafa Khān, whose servants are daily pressing Mr. Hicks, the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-153" data-page="153" data-folio="115" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n152_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 115">[p. 115]</button> chief factor, for payment; he, having no means himself, has applied in turn to Agent Farren at Goa, but the latter ‘professeth to bee in the like or worse condicion. So that it is to bee feared missery will in short time befall such of Mr. Courteenes servants as are engaged amongst the Moores, when enevitably an exterordinary dishonour to our nacion and prejudice to your trade must follow, if some course bee not taken to release them’. Know not what lading the <em>Sun</em> is carrying to England, but it is not likely to be much. The <em>Ruth</em> has been at Gombroon, where she trucked her rice and pepper for dates, and is now returning to Rājāpur. Captain Smart has died at Achin.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-055" href="#sec-05-fn-055" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-055" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> The <em>Endeavour</em> is about to start for the Coast of Coromandel and Pegu. ‘The newse our last gave you touching the Tarters conquest of China is since come confirmed from Goa, a pynnace of Cocheene being returned from Machaw empty, that trade for the present being wholly lost, the Portugalls which the passed yeare went from Goa haveing found no vend for their goods they carried nor any merchandize procurable to returne.’ <em>PS. (April</em> 1)—The <em>Supply</em> has just arrived from Rājāpur with the expected saltpetre, &amp;c. When she left that port (March 20), the <em>Sun</em> was preparing to depart for England. What Thompson in the <em>Ruth</em> will do on his return from Persia they know not, the six or seven thousand pounds he left for investment at Rāybāg having been seized in satisfaction of Courteen’s debts; but they fear that this may prompt him ‘to take some indirect courses’. (5½ <em>pp. Received overland December 6.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">President Aaron Baker, Henry Greenhill, and Matthew Nowell at Bantam to the President and Council at Surat</span>, [<span class="sc">March</span>?] 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">a</span>, p. 165).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Now answer the Surat letter of last October, brought by a Dutch vessel. Regret Breton’s ill health and trust that he will soon recover. Perceive that the Manilla trade ‘is at a period for the present’, and whether any further attempt will be possible is very uncertain. The Dutch at Batavia are preparing a fleet of twenty ships for dispatch thither, to be ready by the middle of the present month, and give out that they ‘will never desist till they have made the place theire <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-154" data-page="154" data-folio="116" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n153_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 116">[p. 116]</button> owne’. Wish that the news received at Goa of a peace between the King and the Parliament had proved true; but the intelligence brought by the last ships is to a different effect. A letter received from Gilbert Grimes, master of the <em>Greyhound</em>, dated at the Mauritius on November 30, announces that he reached that island on September 28, his vessel, being slow of sail, having lost the monsoon for Surat. He does not say how long he is to remain there, but presumably he will not reach Surat until about the end of next September. Cannot understand why he did not go on either to Bantam or to the Coromandel Coast. The loss of his voyage is evidently due to the Company buying a rotten old vessel instead of building a new one, which would do them five times the service. Of the ships dispatched to Bantam, the <em>William</em> arrived on August 27, the <em>Endymion</em> two days later, and the <em>Ulysses</em> on September 8. Lists of their cargoes are enclosed. The first-named vessel has been sent out on account of the Joint Stock; the other two for the General Voyage, to fetch home the goods remaining here on that account. Cartwright’s letters have led the Company to imagine that these goods will suffice to lade home both vessels, but in this they will be disappointed. Have managed, however, to find cargoes for the <em>William</em> and the <em>Ulysses</em>, which sailed accordingly on January (?) 6. The Surat packet for Manilla is returned herewith unopened; and Boyer, together with some petty things he brought with him, will be sent back in the next ship. Enclose three letters received from England for delivery to Courteen’s factors. Again urge the dispatch of a ship from Surat to fetch such goods as are here available; otherwise they fear it will be impossible to forward any supply. <em>PS.</em>—Hearing of a Dutch ship bound for the Mauritius, they have written a brief letter to Grimes, a copy of which is enclosed. If he brings his ship to Bantam, as desired, they promise not to take out any cargo destined to Surat but to send her on at once, after putting their pepper aboard. (<em>Copy.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Revett Walwyn at Broach to the President and Council at Surat, April</span> 15, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">A</span>, pp. 117, 121).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has this day sent off 64 bales of cloth. For the cost he refers to the enclosed invoice. Doubtless more is expected, but he has not <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-155" data-page="155" data-folio="117" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n154_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 117">[p. 117]</button> been able to induce the dyers and beaters to work any faster. Will do his best to send off a further quantity shortly. Requests instructions as to the dyeing, &amp;c., of certain cloths. <em>PS.</em>—The workmen promise to finish all they have in hand within five days. (<em>Copy.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">John Spiller and Nicholas Scrivener at Kandiāro to the Same, April</span> 17, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">A</span>, pp. 142, 130–8, 142).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Having got together some money, they left Tatta by water on February 2 [    ], and reached Nasarpur on March 3. On their way they received a letter from Ahmadābād, enclosing a bill for 15,000 rupees; and this money was brought to them on March 8 by Henry Garry. On viewing the cloth at Nasarpur, they found it good, and cheaper than last year by 8 pice per piece. However, they were not contented with it, and at once ‘caused the weavers to alter theire loomes and weave cloth one-sixth part better, for before the warp was of 600 threds and now have made it 700; the breadth the same as formerly, which is a Guzzeratt covet. Soe questionless this cloth will give content and be better liked off then ever yet any went thence. And hope shall procure good quantities, for the weavers (being noe buyers but us) very willingly and readily condescended to our desire’. On March 9 the factors set out again, and on the 24th they arrived at ‘the Gate of Derbellah <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-056" href="#sec-05-fn-056" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-056" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’, where their broker met them. He reported that cloth had been extraordinarily dear for some time, owing to purchases made by a Bukkur merchant, but prices had now fallen again. The factors continued in the town some time, but could get but little cloth—about two corge a day, at which rate ‘our buzzare <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-057" href="#sec-05-fn-057" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-057" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> continues’. ‘The Multan prince, Moraad Buxe<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-058" href="#sec-05-fn-058" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-058" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>, being outed of Multan, his whole gover[nment] was given to Seyhed Ckawne [Said Khān], who sent three of [his so]nnes to Buker <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-059" href="#sec-05-fn-059" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-059" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> to take care and charge of that place, having given them [it?] with all the depending [tow]nes, which together are devided into three purgaines [i.e. parganas] for there maintanance <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-156" data-page="156" data-folio="118" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n155_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 118">[p. 118]</button> by theire father; about the shareing of which theire was some time spent before they could agree. But that being ended, this place [i.e. Kandiāro], Derbellah, and Gumbutt<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-060" href="#sec-05-fn-060" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-060" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> fell to the eldest sonne, Canahzaut Ckawne [Khānahzād Khān]; the only places of cloath or that we have any thinge to doe in. Yet that unreasonable dutie of Cheheleaheeck<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-061" href="#sec-05-fn-061" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-061" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> was alloted the second sonne. Soe here tis hard to judge who is governour; for Seyed Ckawn, beinge by the Kinge (who againe is on his journey for Black [i.e. Balkh]) called unto him, sent for his eldest sonne to governe Multan in his absence; who in his place hath left soe many governours that hitherto we cannot learne each’s authorrity. These came to receive in theire rents the same day we came to Derbellah, but durst not [go?] noe further then Khandearah, the arbaubs<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-062" href="#sec-05-fn-062" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-062" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> and cheife men being all fledd, standing out as rebbells, and soe they saide they would continue, untill the governours aforesaid would condiscend to theire demands; which at last with much adoe were agreed on both sides; and then the governours left this place and went for Derbellah (to our soe much content as [the] weavers rejoycing), where the arbhaubs had promised to meete with them.’ On their departure the factors sent their servants to all the neighbouring villages to hasten in the cloth, ‘for from these places comes in the halfe of what wee buy’. The Kotwāl, discovering this, one day laid hold of some of those who had sold cloth to the English, demanding ‘laggath<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-063" href="#sec-05-fn-063" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-063" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup>’ on the money they had received and threatening to make them pay duties on the cloth they had brought to sell, notwithstanding that such a tax had never been levied before, and that the factors had already paid two duties on the money, one in Darbēlo and the other in this place ‘at the Chowtralls<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-064" href="#sec-05-fn-064" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-064" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup>’. They insisted, therefore, that the Kotwāl should release the weavers and return the money he had seized; this at last he did, but at the same time threatened to enforce his demands in future. The factors had previously had trouble with him, and had then got him turned out of office for a time; they have <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-157" data-page="157" data-folio="119" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n156_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 119">[p. 119]</button> now made a fresh complaint and are promised that he shall be dismissed for good. Have received a further supply of moneys by bills from Ahmadābād. Note that all their letters have duly reached Surat. The Shāhbandar dispatched the Prince’s new vessel to Mokha, but she was damaged in a storm and was forced to put back. The cloth they buy is of the ordinary bazaar length. They could probably get some of different dimensions, if required, as the weavers are anxious for work; but it would be dearer and no considerable quantity could be procured, for the weavers will only make special lengths so long as they are forced to do so. Garry reports from Nasarpur that the cloth investment is progressing favourably. Hope to get 100 corge a month there, as there are so few buyers. ‘Wee were noe sooner arrived at Sunne [<em>see the 1637–41 volume</em>, p. 136] in our way hither but all the arbhaubs and indico makers in that place came to welcome us, much desiring that we would leave a man there, for that they should have halfe soe [much] againe as last yeare; which they saide wee [could] perceive by the addicion of new wheeles that they had sett [upon] the river to bring up water to there [grounds].’ Having noticed that this was so, they encouraged them as much as possible, desiring them to make their indigo better than this; so they hope to procure a good and plentiful supply. Have not offered any advance to the saltpetre maker, he being indeed unwilling to receive any, ‘by reason another saltpeter man informed the Governor that he made for us, of whom he should doe well to demand laggah; so hereuppon [he] was imprisoned, and after paying a little money got cleare’. Have written to Nasarpur about indigo; but for the present ‘the cheife time for its making is spent’. The baftas will be dyed in Tatta; the cloth itself they intend to buy in Gambat. Will inquire as to the possibilities of obtaining freight at port; but the merchants are much discouraged. While the factors were there, the Prince’s ship sank off the customhouse one night, when fully laden; and when she was got up, her cargo had all to be ‘cured’ again. Then came her recent mischance. A further subject of grievance is ‘Bumbaes brothers ill usage; for its saide the King have fined him 100,000 rupees, which is a paying in Tattah; besides which the Princes and other great mens fees will carry away at least 50,000 rupees more. This all men exclaime againe[st], being the worst (they say) of all ill customes that <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-158" data-page="158" data-folio="120" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n157_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 120">[p. 120]</button> ever came upp yett; for now, if a merchant of fashion, or any other that hath but meanes, dyes, his wealth will be all seized on, though he hath children and brothers to leave it unto, and, haveing soe, the Kinge ought not pretend any right’. Money received from Ahmadābād for this year’s investment. More will be required before the end of August; but if possible they will obtain this by drawing bills of exchange. Could not avoid taking back the broadcloth returned by the Shāhbandar, as he had done them many courtesies. Part of what he had he passed on to the Governor, but it was not paid for by the latter by the time of his death, which happened about March 20. ‘Soe his goods being all seized and sealed by the Kings officers (as the manner is when any umbraw deceases), our broad cloth amoungst the rest was mett withall; but we, haveing a baraatt<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-065" href="#sec-05-fn-065" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-065" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, and the Shabunder betweene, doubt not of its recoverye.’ Mahābat Khān’s second son<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-066" href="#sec-05-fn-066" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-066" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, now in Gujarāt, has been granted the vacant post; and the King has sent an officer to see that none of Mīr Khān’s effects be carried away—a poor reward for his long service. The news of bad markets in Persia will still further discourage the Tatta merchants and cause them to abstain from buying goods. Their agents here have gone away, after selling their pice to the English merchants. Notwithstanding, the price of cloth is very little less than last year; but the weavers have now shown more willingness to accept advances. The ordinary bazaar cloth is, however, much cheaper; if any is wanted, word should be sent at once, but at present the price is as low in Tatta as here, or even lower. The death of the Governor has invalidated ‘the dustack [<em>see</em> p. 58] he gave us for freing us from the irkesome dutie of water rawdaere [<em>rāhdārī</em>, transit duty]; which now at our comeing forth we got reneued, being that some of the officers of the Ghaat began to trouble our boatemen. Soe if our goods goes downe before a new governour arives, it will receive much perturbation, besides the extreordinarye charges; for now uppon every place where duties are received sett both the Dewans servants and the deaceaseds aforesaid, as we were informed by the Drogah<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-067" href="#sec-05-fn-067" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-067" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> of Chobarrghaat not long since.’ <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-159" data-page="159" data-folio="121" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n158_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 121">[p. 121]</button> Were it not for this, they would send down a boat’s load of cloth, for the Governor of ‘Sevestaune’, whom they visited on their way up, has promised to respect the <em>dastak</em>. Spiller hopes to be back in Tatta about the middle of June. <em>PS.</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-068" href="#sec-05-fn-068" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-068" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>—Encloses an application from Scrivener for an increase of salary, and trusts it will be granted. (<em>Copy. Original received May</em> 8.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Nicholas Scrivener at Kandiāro to the President and Council at Surat, April</span> [17?], 1647<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-069" href="#sec-05-fn-069" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-069" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">a</span>, p. 143).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Reminds them that his covenanted period of service, viz. three years, at 20<em>l.</em> has long since expired, and begs that he may be granted an increased remuneration, which he will endeavour to deserve. Refers to the general letter for what has occurred since they left Tatta. (<em>Copy. Original received May</em> 8.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">George Tash, [Hugh] Fenn, and [Anthony] Smith at Ahmadābād to the Same, April</span> 22, 1647 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 125).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Forward letters from the factors at Mokha, received from a Diu banyan. The Dutch made a provisional bargain for indigo, but it seems to have been disallowed by their chief at Surat. Suggest that an arrangement be made with them to buy about 600 maunds together and then share it. <em>PS.</em>—Request a supply of paper. (<em>Copy. Original received April</em> 28.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Revett Walwyn at Broach to the Same, April</span> 24, 1647 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 124).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Advising dispatch of certain piece-goods. Others will be ready shortly. (<em>Copy. Original received April</em> 26.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Edward Knipe, Francis Hammersley, and Richard Davidge at Agra to the Same, April</span> 24, 1647 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 149).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">[<em>First part missing.</em>] Provision of sugar (?) and saltpetre. Fear that 1,200 maunds will be their limit as regards the latter; ‘the begetters being extreamly troubled by the Kings officers, who forbid boyling <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-160" data-page="160" data-folio="122" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n159_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 122">[p. 122]</button> for any but the Kings cirkar<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-070" href="#sec-05-fn-070" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-070" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>; yet, by corrupting with small bribes, we should finde little difficultye in procureing it, were the time of the yeare fitt for the action.’ Thought it best to send the caravan by way of Ahmadābād, because there was no Englishman at Burhānpur to look after it. Hope to buy stick-lac and gum-lac in about a month’s time. All the bills of exchange received have been duly cashed, except part of those last to hand. Concerning these, there is a dispute as to the money in which payment is to be made; and, to avoid further controversy on this point, they beg that in future all bills may ‘runne in Shaw Jehaun rupees’. The Lucknow factors have had 70,000 rupees, while here nearly a lakh has been disbursed; further remittances will therefore be necessary. Have succeeded in reducing the rate of interest on their borrowings to three-quarters per cent. per month. Have inquired into the shortage discovered at Ahmadābād in some of the piece-goods sent down last year, and find that it was due to the fact that ‘the Agra covett by the Kings comand uppon his last departure was made equall to the Lahoare covett, being better then ⅓ of a gerrae<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-071" href="#sec-05-fn-071" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-071" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> shorter then formerly, the difference computated at least 2½ per cent.’. ‘The musters of indico sent with the rymes<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-072" href="#sec-05-fn-072" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-072" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> by generall report is made about Gwalleir, 40 course [<em>kos</em>] distant from this place Brampore way; whose substance, when extracted from the leafe, not being sufficiently conglutenous to be made into such guttees<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-073" href="#sec-05-fn-073" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-073" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> usuall in Byanah and other places and continue the same firme when dry, but doth crumble and suddainely breake into small peeces, therefore doe the makers incorporate therewith rice water to make it hold together; which is the reason it is soe hard and flinty. It is sayde by these dyers to be a bad and base sort of indico, of little [             ] difficult in the workeing.’ Express their thanks for the news sent them. Knipe desires to learn unto whom he is to deliver up his accounts; also permission to arrange for coming to Surat. (<em>Copy. Original received May</em> 24.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-161"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="161" data-folio="123" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n160_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 123">[p. 123]</button></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Edward Pearce</span> [<span class="sc">at Swally Marine</span>] <span class="sc">to the President at Surat</span>, <span class="sc">April</span> 25 (?), 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">A</span>, p. 122).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Account of goods still on board the <em>Supply</em>. Planks wanted for her use. Griffin thinks that, if it could be spared, ‘the new jallewott [<em>see the preceding volume</em>, p. 197] that is made at Surratt’ would be very useful to that vessel in the Straits of Malacca. Forgot to advise that Cherry put on shore some of the Bombay rice, declaring that he had no room for it. ‘Serias’ [<em>see the previous volume</em>, p. 130] wanted for sails; also some powder. <em>PS.</em>—Desires some ‘salloes<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-074" href="#sec-05-fn-074" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-074" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’ for ensigns and flags. (<em>Copy.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Robert Heynes and John Lewis at Gombroon to the President and Council at Surat</span> [<span class="sc">April</span> 25?] 1647<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-075" href="#sec-05-fn-075" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-075" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 152).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Answer the letter from Surat brought by the Dutch <em>Snoek</em>. Regret that it came too late to prevent the sale of their pepper. Note that certain ‘falce abcs’ [<em>abbāsīs</em>] &amp;c. have been recharged to this factory; doubt not to make the broker allow for them. Of Sind goods they desire only half the quantity sent this year, but these must be fine and good. Will keep Surat informed of the state of the markets, which of late have proved to be very bad. The <em>Antelope</em> is now unladen, and will go over to Larak for ballast and water. They have little or no hope at present of freight for her, but will do their best. As directed, they will remit by her 15,000 rials of eight; but they are troubled as to the specie in which to send that sum. ‘Ryalls by all relacion is all the mony that is sent thither; but they are soe scarce now in towne that few or none are to be had. For new abcs, if you meane those of 10 graines, they are not procureable; if they be, we shall endeavour to gett them. Therefore, if we faile of these two speties, new mony of 9 graines or turpora or chickines [sequins] must make oup the summe.’ Will be careful not to detain the vessel longer than is necessary. Promise to treat Codrington with all friendly respect. Enclose copy of their invoice per <em>Delftshaven</em>. On <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-162" data-page="162" data-folio="124" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n161_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 124">[p. 124]</button> March 26 they received letters, money, and goods from Wilton; the money will be sent to Masulipatam. Four days later came another letter from him, enclosing a new farmān from the King for the shipment of their horses, and intimating that he had written them by Dutch conveyance. As this missive has not reached them, they fear it is detained by the Hollanders to prevent their proceedings at Ispahān from being known. On March 28 arrived the Dutch <em>Liefde</em> from Masulipatam. List of the goods she brought and the prices they realized. Particulars of their own sales and the amount produced. Wish that an English ship were here, bound for Surat, as they could easily have procured freight for her. News of one of Courteen’s vessels. Still intend to transmit the ‘guldares’ to Basra for sale. The nākhudā of a Masulipatam junk arrived here reported that he had been robbed of a quantity of cloth by two Portuguese ships, bound for Europe; he exhibited the Viceroy’s pass, but this was disregarded, as were the protests of a number of Dutchmen on board. <em>PS.</em>—The [<em>Antelope</em>?] returned from Larak on the 22nd, having with some difficulty obtained sufficient ballast. She could get no water there, and so the factors have had to provide her with a supply here. She will sail either to-day or to-morrow. Yesterday the Dutch ‘cossett’ brought letters from Wilton, advising that ‘on the 11th [ultimo? two Dut]ch comissaries<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-076" href="#sec-05-fn-076" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-076" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> arived at Spahane, who [           ]inge with 500 ashruphees<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-077" href="#sec-05-fn-077" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-077" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, 5½ pieces of broad[cloth           ] and a cloke. [          ]ence as performed nothing; but, as he is in[formed they] desire silk for 35 or 40 tomand per load. [          ]theire was about. One of them told Etta[mon] Dow[lett that they] desired to be merchants, not souldiers in the [Kings coun]try; to whom Curche Bassau<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-078" href="#sec-05-fn-078" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-078" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> returned an[nswer that they] comenced acts of hostillity.’ (<em>Copy.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Edward Pearce [at Swally Marine] to the President at Surat, April</span> 26, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">a</span>, p. 124).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Six bales of white cloth have been found on board and will be sent up. Desires instructions as to the goods which are to go to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-163" data-page="163" data-folio="125" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n162_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 125">[p. 125]</button> Surat in the boats. Deceit of ‘Nanaby’ [Nānābhāi] regarding the ‘coles’ [i. e. charcoal]. Powder and shot wanted for the <em>Supply</em>. (<em>Copy.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Revett Walwyn at Broach to the President and Council at Surat, April</span> 27, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">A</span>, p. 128).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Sends down a last batch of piece-goods and desires instructions whether he is to remain here. (<em>Copy. Original received April</em> 29.)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Samuel Wilton at Ispahān to the Company, April</span> 30, 1647 (<em>O.C.</em> 2035).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Particulars of goods sold since Heynes left this city on December 17; the proceeds were sent to Gombroon, arriving on March 24, four days after the <em>Francis</em> had sailed. The <em>Expedition</em> and <em>Lanneret</em> reached that port in December: the <em>Falcon</em> on January 13: the <em>Endeavour</em> and <em>Seahorse</em> on February 6: and the <em>Antelope</em> on April 4. ‘The 2d January arrived the India embassador in very greate state, yett altogeither slighted of the King and as yett hath not any satisfactory answer unto his embassadge, it being altogeither displeasing to the King, the Mogull requireing three considerable places, viz. Meshett, Herree, Farrar<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-079" href="#sec-05-fn-079" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-079" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>; soe that it is very probable they will wage warre one with other.
