Sugar is scarce and dear, the Governor’s tyranny having deterred the merchants from bringing any down. Instructions desired as to buying more cotton yarn. Goods for Bantam. The washing of cloth has been hindered by want of water in the river. [<em>Part illegible.</em>] Isaiah Joyce returns thanks for the favour shown him, and will endeavour to deserve it. (<em>Copy. Original received September</em> 18.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Hicks, Walter Littleton, Thomas Gough, and Abraham Aldington at Rāybāg to the Same, September</span> 15, 1647 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 257).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have certain news that Thompson is providing himself with ‘friggatts’, &amp;c., for the taking of junks and the plundering of small coast towns; and that, if he cannot prevail here, he means to lie at the mouth of the Red Sea and capture ‘the Magull junckes’ and any others he may light upon. If this be not prevented, it ‘will cost us our lives and Your Worships much trouble’; and so they implore that something may be done. Thompson will be found between Dābhol and Kārwār, ‘if you shalbe pleased to send strenght to quench this fire.’ The factor at Rājāpur, Mr. ‘Demasters’, will give them every assistance; but Lloyd, at Kārwār, favours Thompson, although he pretends otherwise. At present they are indebted here 10,720 ‘pagodas greate’, though the debt is much lessened by the goods that have been seized from them, belonging to ‘Alderman Andrews and Company’. The debt is at interest at 13½ per cent., ‘which deeply eateth’; and they therefore beg assistance to wipe off their indebtedness, and so either go home ‘or else serve you, if soe you shall bee pleased’. Courteen has charged <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-194" data-page="194" data-folio="156" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n193_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 156">[p. 156]</button> them to persist in demanding the repayment of the money saved in the wreck of the <em>William</em> and made over to the Company’s servants at Madraspatam; this sum would set them free, if the President and Council would be good enough to pay it. Will write again shortly. The Mokha junks belonging to Rājāpur have now returned, and so have escaped Thompson. <em>PS.</em>—Forward a letter addressed by Thompson to the Governor of this place. (<em>Copy. Original received September 30.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Henry Benn</span>, aboard the <em>Hind</em> [<span class="sc">at Swally</span>], to the <span class="sc">President at Surat, September 18, 1647</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">a</span>, p. 218).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Anchored here this afternoon. Left Bantam on July 20. Of their crew of forty, two are dead and many sick or weak. Was delayed by a storm off Sanjān. Account of the cargo. A cable much needed. (<em>Copy.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Revett Walwyn at Broach to the President and Council at Surat, September 20, 1647</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 227).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Fears that the Dutch will greatly impede his operations, as they have orders to buy a considerable quantity of cloth, and prices are rising accordingly. Details his own proceedings, here and at Ankleswar. Hopes to procure more cloth next month, ‘it being the Moores Rammazan<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-098" href="#sec-05-fn-098" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-098" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, when likely they sitt (?) closer to worke then at other times, by reason they feast not soe often.’ Has forwarded the letter intended for ‘Cassidas’ [Kāsī Dās]. <em>PS.</em>—‘Beeves’ [i. e. oxen] cost here 7½ or 8 rupees each, and would then be inferior to those previously bought. Advises recourse to some cheaper place. (<em>Copy.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">George Tash, Hugh Fenn, and Anthony Smith at Ahmadābād to the Same, September 23, 1647</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 242).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Governor has taken over from the Dutch their saltpetre, repaying them what they had disbursed thereon. Note the instructions from Surat to contract privately for it; but the ‘Borahs’ <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-195" data-page="195" data-folio="157" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n194_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 157">[p. 157]</button> [<em>see</em> p. 127] are jointly engaged with the Dutch in the business and from them only the ‘utmost opposition’ is to be expected; while there are no other dealers in the commodity with whom it would be possible to make a private arrangement. As ordered, they will refrain from all public buying of saltpetre until further directions reach them. It is said that the Dutch mean to make a further offer at a higher price. Intend to draw on Surat for 10,000 rupees. There are various rumours of the impending removal of the Governor; it is hoped that the King’s arrival at ‘Dilly’ will be followed by such a change. Have been busy with the indigo investment at Sarkhej, and doubt not soon to make up their purchases to the 400 bales required. Cannot yet conclude the bargain for the 100 bales of Agra indigo, as the owner is standing out for a better price. The Governor lately sent a second parwāna to Mīrzā Alī Akbar, ‘requiring the release of his horse’, and so they expect his demand will now be complied with. Sent 7,000 rupees to Tatta on July 22, and a further 5,000 on August 15; have now, in conformity with orders from Surat, forwarded 3,000 more to complete the investment. <em>PS.</em>—As the exchange runs high, they have not yet taken up any bills. The drought between Merta and ‘Seroy<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-099" href="#sec-05-fn-099" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-099" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’ has made the ways impassable; so it is feared that the caravan from Agra will have more trouble than usual. (<em>Copy. Original received September</em> 29.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Stevens, aboard the</span> <em>EAGLE</em> <span class="sc">in Swally Road, to the Same, [September]</span> 24, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">A</span>, p. 229).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">His ship, after leaving Swally on January 4, 1646, passed Johanna on the 27th ditto, and anchored on February 25 in ‘Table Bay’, where she stayed fifteen days, waiting in vain for the <em>Mary</em>. On March 24 they sighted St. Helena, where lay eight Dutch ships bound for Europe; but, owing to the currents and a fresh wind off shore, the <em>Eagle</em> was unable to get in and so was forced to proceed to Ascension. There they stayed one night ‘and tooke 22 greate turkles, which proved as good refreshing for our men as soe many beeves’; and then on April 2 they departed. They arrived in the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-196" data-page="196" data-folio="158" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n195_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 158">[p. 158]</button> Downs on May 26, without having lost more than one man, who died at the Cape. With regard to their present voyage, the <em>Eagle</em> and <em>Blessing</em> reached the Downs on March 28 [1647]; but, having orders to see the <em>Mary</em> out of the Channel, they did not get away until April 19, only to be driven back into the Downs by a storm. On April 27, they sailed again, losing sight of England on May 3. The Canaries were passed on the 22nd, and on June 19 they crossed the Line. Cape Agulhas was sighted on July 27, and Madagascar on August 8. Eight days later they reached Johanna, where they found a letter written by Grimes, announcing that he had lost his passage to India the previous year: that Robert Tindall had left this place for the Coast on April [<em>should be</em> May] 27: and that Grimes himself was departing for Surat on August 10. Nothing was heard of John or Thomas Proud [<em>see</em> p. 131]: so possibly they too have missed the monsoon, and have therefore gone to Bantam. Hopes that the <em>Eagle</em> will be enabled to start on her return voyage before Christmas. Regrets that he is not so well furnished as before with stores and provisions for the use of the factory. The ship is in want of ropes, and her stock of food is low. The Company provisioned her only for fourteen months for the double voyage, and at first allowed only ninety men; while in the Downs this number was increased to a hundred, but there are so many youths and boys among them that the vessel was never worse manned. (<em>Copy. Original received September</em> 25.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Broadbent</span>, aboard the <em>Blessing</em> [in Swally Road] to the President at Surat, September 24, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">a</span>, p. 232).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Narrates the outward voyage of the two ships. The <em>Mary</em> left them on May 28. Next day they sighted Cape Verd, and a consultation was held, at which it was decided that, as the time of year was late, they should separate, meeting again at Johanna; but there was little or no difference in their rates of sailing, and they did not part until after crossing the Line on June 19. Twenty-seven days later they met again, and have ever since kept company. They left Johanna on August 27. He is keeping the Company’s packet until the President comes down to the Marine. (<em>Copy. Original received September</em> 25.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-197"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="197" data-folio="159" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n196_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 159">[p. 159]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Hicks, Walter Littleton, Thomas Gough, and Abraham Aldington at Rāybāg to the President and Council at Surat, September</span> 24, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">a</span>, p. 265).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The enclosed letters from Kārwār show that no admonishment will produce any effect on Thompson. If in consequence of his proceedings they lose their lives, they beg that ‘you would bee pleased to give timely notice to the Parliament, that such actions may have their rewarde’. Would be glad to receive any letters that may have arrived from Europe for them. (<em>Copy. Original received October</em> 11.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Gilbert Grimes [aboard the <em>Greyhound</em>] in Swally Road to the President at Surat, September</span> 26, 1647 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 234).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Rejoices to announce his arrival. Encloses a copy of a letter he sent from Johanna by the <em>Farewell</em> [<em>see</em> p. 131]. (<em>Copy. Original received same day</em>.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">[<span class="sc">John Spiller at Tatta] to the President and Council at Surat [September</span> 30?], 1647 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 266).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Since writing on the 8th present, he has received by way of Ahmadābād a further letter from Surat, to which he now replies. As regards the future provision of piece-goods, it will not do to trust much to Nasarpur, for it is too near Tatta. ‘Besides, those weavers are a company of base rougues, for, notwithstanding wee give them mony aforehand [      ] part of the yeare, and that in the time of there greatest want, yet, if any pedling cloth merchant comes to buy, they leave us and worke for him, though hee gives noe money aforehande; beeing the ordinary base make is more facill and easy to weave then ours, with which they must take some paines.’ It is absolutely necessary, therefore, to continue the investment in Kandiāro, in order to keep the Nasarpur weavers up to the mark. Cannot understand the Company’s complaint that the Kandiāro cloth is only 12 yards long. All that he has measured here is longer than that; so he thinks some mistake has been made. [<em>The rest is wanting.</em>] (<em>Copy.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-198"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="198" data-folio="160" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n197_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 160">[p. 160]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">John Adler at Swally Marine to the President at Surat, September</span> 31 (<em>sic</em>), 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">a</span>, p. 259).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Gives an account of arrack delivered to the various ships. <em>PS.</em>—Begs that orders may be issued for provisioning the <em>Seahorse</em> for her voyage. (<em>Copy. Original received October</em> 1.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">George Tash, Hugh Fenn, and Anthony Smith at Ahmadābād to the President and Council at Surat, October</span> 4, 1647 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 260).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The quantity of indigo available was so small that, although there has been no competition either from the Dutch or from the dyers, they found it very difficult to complete their 100 bales of the round sort; ‘and before wee have finished 250 of the rury [<em>see</em> p. 31], wee assure ourselves wee shall not leave 100 maunds of that might bee worth our owneing unbought.’ ‘Although wee finde not a proportionable difference betweene the fineness of it and the flatt to equall the price, yett the cleaness of the round, which is voyde of all sand sticking to it, tis of greater moment then wee could have possibly imagined, and causeth a difference of weight of neare four seare in a maund; which deceipt the indico makers practized to there benifitt, laying the flatt sorte upon sand wett,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-100" href="#sec-05-fn-100" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-100" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> which sticking to it is soe fastened by the sunes drying it as that it remaines at weighing.’ As soon as the price of indigo leaf is brisk, they will be in a position to calculate the cost of manufacture; but they dare not promise ‘to make of the flatt sorte, pure and good, for 20 rupees, since the leafe is saide to bee shorte in quantitie’, compared with last year. ‘Our chints and tappichindaes, which were the most considerable parte of our Bantam investment, were delivered to the workemen soe seasonably that they were all returned painted before the raines; soe that there was as then nothing wanting unto them but washing, which might have beene performed in eight daies time (they being to that purpose returned <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-199" data-page="199" data-folio="161" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n198_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 161">[p. 161]</button> to them upon cessation of the raines), but our Governor not suffering them to worke in the river hath beene an exceeding impediment to the bussines, whilst the chinters have neglected there worke to attend upon the durbbar and sollicite redress, which for consideracion of 250 rupees was once granted to them; to the payment whereof they had noe sooner consented then our base, unjust, and worthless Governor raysed the summe to a 1000.’ They are, notwithstanding, confident that these goods will be at Rānder by November 5; though probably the investment for next season will be much delayed. The Dutch are in a like predicament, having in their house vast quantities of cloth which they had intended to make into ‘chints’, &amp;c. If the ‘chinters’ either compound with the Governor or agree to work at ‘Mummadavad<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-101" href="#sec-05-fn-101" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-101" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’, the factors will do their best to keep ahead of the Dutch, in order that their goods may be brought to market earlier. Have now no chests worth sending down, as those that were formerly available, being wormeaten, have been used for firewood or other purposes. Note the goods returned from Mokha. From the specimens of ‘puttolaes’ sent, they now understand how what the Bantam factors call ‘puttola gobars<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-102" href="#sec-05-fn-102" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-102" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>’ should be made; but the workmen here, in consideration of the unusual breadth, demand a third more than is paid for the ordinary kind; so, unless they will reduce their rates, inquiry will be made at Cambay or ‘Pattan<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-103" href="#sec-05-fn-103" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-103" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>’. Are surprised to learn that their cotton yarn scarcely equals that bought at Surat for price and goodness; will endeavour ‘to receive it better then the musters; otherwise the way charges and Barroch customes will render it to deare to give content unto our honourable employers’. Enumerate the sums they have remitted to Tatta, to show that the factors there complain without reason of their backwardness to supply the necessary funds. <em>PS.</em>—Have just heard that indigo leaf has much fallen in price, ‘by reason of the fayleing of most of the makers’; so that now 45 seers are offered for a rupee, as against 32½ and 33 last year. This they conceive will much further their <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-200" data-page="200" data-folio="162" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n199_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 162">[p. 162]</button> proposal to make a good quantity, perhaps as much as is likely to be needed for next year’s return. The quicksilver merchants here offer to buy all that may arrive this year at higher rates than ‘your Surrat bazar’. (<em>Copy. Original received October 9</em>.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">President Breton and Messrs. Merry, Fitch, and Pearce at Surat to the Company, October</span> 7, 1647 (<em>O.C.</em> 2045).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Company’s letter of December 23, 1646 (with transcript of one dated September 18) arrived here on August 6, having been brought to the Coast by the <em>Farewell</em> on July 2; and their subsequent letters of March 31, sent by the <em>Eagle</em> and <em>Blessing</em>, came to hand September 25, when those two ships anchored in Swally Hole, followed the next day by the <em>Greyhound</em>. For an account of the voyages of these vessels, they refer to the commanders’ letters, sent herewith. The <em>Hind</em> arrived from Bantam on September 18; and two days later the <em>Seahorse</em> came in from Aden, having brought back part of her cargo—‘a sufficient testimony of dead marketts, both there and at Mocho’. The late arrival of the <em>Antelope</em> and the non-appearance of the <em>Greyhound</em> made it impossible to send those two vessels back to England, as ordered, last season; and in any case such a course was out of the question, owing to the difficulty of procuring the necessary cargoes in time. Now, however, they have a good stock of saltpetre &amp;c. in hand, and hope to dispatch both those ships and the <em>Eagle</em> some time in December, ‘if Capt. Thompson, commander of the <em>Ruth</em>, who hath resolved to reprize the Decan jouncks for some meanes of his attached by those merchants for sattisfaction of Mr. Courteens debts, disturbe not our trade in those parts.’ They are expecting pepper and cardamoms from thence, and without these goods they will not be able to fill the three vessels; so they have sent a protest to Thompson, and are now dispatching the <em>Blessing</em> to Rājāpur ‘to pry into his actions’. She is to proceed thence to ‘Mirchee<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-104" href="#sec-05-fn-104" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-104" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> and soe downe as low as Batticola’ [Bhatkal], to buy pepper and cardamoms, for which purpose her commander, William Broadbent, has been furnished with a sum of money, and a broker has been sent to assist him. Oxenden has gone in the <em>Falcon</em> from Mokha to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-201" data-page="201" data-folio="163" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n200_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 163">[p. 163]</button> Cape Comorin and those parts to procure pepper and cinnamon; ‘but of the latter we can enterteine but litle hopes, the Vice Roy haveing exceedeing strictly inhibited the sale of that commodity, whereof lately they can procure but very litle for themselves.’ The <em>Antelope</em> left Gombroon on April 25 and reached the Coast June 7. The Masulipatam factors promise to dispatch her by September 10. The <em>Endeavour</em> and <em>Francis</em> sailed on April 18 for the Coast, ‘full laden with rice, which arrived very seasonably to supply Fort St. George; and what they had not occassion to use sold at great rates.’ From thence the former departed for Pegu about the end of September. Now that the <em>Farewell</em> has arrived, it is presumed that the Coast factors will send the <em>Francis</em> to Bantam. The <em>Supply</em> has gone to Achin and Johore. ‘The Dutch, we know not uppon what pretence, not only refuseing to give passes to any jouncks for that port, but have published their resolucions, both here, at the Coast, and other places where their people are resident, to intercept all such shipping as they shall encounter bound thither; but therein we hope their greatnes will exclude us, who as litle respect their threats as themselves.’ Indigo continues very scarce and dear. Captain Thompson has intimated his intention of sailing for England about the middle of this month. (<em>Copy.</em> 3 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Thomas Ivy and William Gurney at Fort St. George to the Same</span>, <span class="sc">October</span> 9, 1647 (<em>O. C.</em> 2046).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Company’s letter of April 6, 1646, was received from Bantam by the pinnace <em>Advice</em> on May 5 last, together with the coral and broadcloth consigned to this Coast in the <em>William</em> and the <em>Ulysses</em>. Cannot dispose of the coral, owing to war and a cruel famine which has now lasted two years. Half the people in this kingdom are dead. No goods will sell, except a little broadcloth for the use of the soldiers. ‘How violent the famine hath bine here tis not to bee credited, for out of the towne of Madraspatam died in five months time 4,000 people; out of Puliccatte 15,000 in as little tyme; and out of St. Th[ome] no less then out of Puliccatte; so that heere is not above ⅓ of the weavers, painters, and wasshers liveinge of what were formerly.’ This has made cloth 15 per cent. dearer than formerly, and little can be procured even at those prices; while European goods are quite unvendible.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-202"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="202" data-folio="164" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n201_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 164">[p. 164]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Disposal of the money brought by the <em>Advice</em>. On May 22 the <em>Endeavour</em> and <em>Francis</em> arrived from Surat with rice, to their great relief; both vessels were then sent to Armagon ‘and a litle to the northward thereof’, to fetch rice for the relief of the inhabitants of Madraspatam, and the freight thus earned has defrayed the charges of both ships while on the Coast. On June 7 the <em>Antelope</em> came in from Gombroon, and two days later was sent on to Masulipatam with the money she brought, to be there invested for Persia. She was ordered to return at once to this place with a lading of rice; but the factors were unable in fifty days to procure more than a fourth of the quantity required, and at last time would not permit of the voyage being made before the date on which the vessel was to leave Masulipatam for Surat. On July 2 the <em>Farewell</em> anchored here, bringing the Company’s letters and some treasure, ‘but not one dropp refreshing in this time of missery, when the least would have bine very acceptable and comfortable unto us, to have washed our heavie harts from the stench of the dead carcazees that lieth most fearefully to beehould in all places that wee goe, as well as the noise of the dyeing people, which can bee noe less terrours to the herers thereof.’ Now reply to the Company’s letter of December 23, 1646. Rejoice to learn that, the Persian and Bantam markets having been fully stocked with Coast ‘clothing’, a quantity has at last found its way to England and there yielded ‘contentable proffitt’. This year they cannot supply either Bantam or the Company, for want of means, owing to the fact that they have nearly 50,000 rials of eight locked up in their coral, ‘which is as dead at present as the dead mens bones that lyeth about our towne.’ Deny that they ever solicited the Surat President to send them a vessel for a voyage to Persia. For seven years he has never failed to dispatch to them a ship and means to be invested for that country; ‘but never induced by us, but rather opposed.’ Know nothing as to the profit made on the <em>Falcon’s</em> voyage thither, as they simply invested the money sent for that purpose from Surat and she never returned to this coast. So far from borrowing that money at dear rates (as is asserted in the Company’s letter), their books have been cleared of any such obligations ‘ever since our first arrivall’. Moreover, the <em>Falcon</em> did not carry any cloth, either of the Bantam stock or on the writers’ <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-203" data-page="203" data-folio="165" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n202_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 165">[p. 165]</button> account. Defend their action in buying the cloth tendered to them by ‘Molay, the Kings cheif agent, and by the Kings seveare order and command unto us’. Had they refused to accept these goods in satisfaction of advances made to the merchants, probably the money would have been lost entirely, and then the factors would equally have been blamed. ‘Soe wee knowe not when wee doe well or ill. Therefore wee beseech the Company, to prevent the like in the future, to send out their orders in all degrees, such as the Dutch hath extent in all their factories, from there Christ cross to their Anperse-and<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-105" href="#sec-05-fn-105" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-105" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>; and questionless wee should indeavour to observe the same as well as they doe.’ Had they not taken this cloth, they would have had none to send either to England or to Bantam; and then they would have been laughed at as Cogan was, ‘when hee was frighted from Portanova from 400 pieces [of] longcloth hee bought there, by a scare crowe, because they said twas the Hollanders cloth<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-106" href="#sec-05-fn-106" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-106" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>’. However, the dispute is now settled. ‘Molay, by many letters of solicitation from the Hollanders Generall of Jaccatra [i. e. Batavia] to Molay, is returned againe to Puliccatte and receaved by the Governor with great honour and respect, and hath justified the sellinge of the goods to us, and our neighbours of Puliccatte as lovinge and seeminge freinds to us as ever formerly hath bine.’ Ivy is willing (since the Company desires it) to remain here some time beyond his covenanted term. Thomas Winter has been appointed from Bantam to succeed to the post of Agent on Ivy’s departure; but, learning that the Company proposes to send out some one to fill the post, he is now discouraged and has applied for permission to proceed to Bantam in the <em>Advice</em>. This, however, could not be granted, owing to the small number of factors at present available. Ivy himself intends to go to Bantam in the <em>Farewell</em> next May, leaving Winter in charge. If the latter will not accept the post, then Gurney must officiate. Of the stock brought out by the <em>Farewell</em>, amounting to 45,932 rials of eight, the gold yielded 13½ per cent. profit and the rials 7 per cent. loss. The <em>Endeavour</em> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-204" data-page="204" data-folio="166" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n203_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 166">[p. 166]</button> left Masulipatam for Pegu on September 15, with a stock of 20,836 rials of eight; the <em>Antelope</em> sailed thence for Surat four days later, with a cargo invoiced at 28,908 rials; and the <em>Advice</em> is now on her departure for Bantam. The <em>Francis</em> is being trimmed at ‘Emaldee’ [<em>see the 1637–41 volume</em>, p. 314], and is intended for dispatch to Bantam next February. Richard Hudson was sent to ‘Bengalah’ last July in the <em>Farewell</em> with 6,000 rials of eight to buy cotton goods and rice. The rest of their stock will be invested in the cargo to be sent to Bantam next May. Have managed to provide for Europe a stock of excellent cloth, though at somewhat dear rates, as it was all bought at Madraspatam, where the famine has raised prices considerably as compared with Masulipatam, which is outside the famine area. There are now only nine factors on the Coast, viz. Ivy, Gurney, and Martin Bradgate here: Thomas Winter, Christopher Yardley, Thomas Chambers, and Edward Winter at Masulipatam and Vīravāsaram; and Richard Hudson and William Netlam in Bengal. As Ivy will be leaving next May, and Thomas Winter desires to return to England, his covenanted time being expired, it will be necessary to recruit the staff with four or six able and honest men. They have only thirty-three soldiers here, and desire that the number be made up to fifty. Enclose a list of the garrison [<em>missing</em>]. ‘Wee had allmost forgotten to advise you that the 16,000 rials of eight President Baker left us indebted at the Coast at his goinge to Bantam was lent us by the King of Gulcondah[s] Gennerall, who hath almost conquer’d this kingdome and reigneth as King under the title of Annabob [<em>see</em> p. 70]. This 16,000 rials hee lent us for one twelve months gratis; which debt wee discharged at the arrivall of the <em>Farewell</em>. So, in requitall of the Annabobs curtezie, wee gave him one of the two brass guns you sent out by the <em>Mary</em>, which hee would not bee denied of, whither hee had lent us this money or no; otherwise hee would not have confirmed our old privilidges formerlye graunted us by the now fledd Jentue King. So upon the deliveringe of this gunn hee gave it us here under his hand that hee received the gunn in full and contentable sattisfaction for the loan of 16,000 rials of eight to the Company the whole space of one twelve month, and never hereafter would desire any thing elce for the same; and withall confirm’d under the King of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-205" data-page="205" data-folio="167" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n204_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 167">[p. 167]</button> Gulcondah[s] great seale all our former privilidges in ample manner, as it was graunted unto us by the foresaid fledd Jentue King. Soe by this meanes the gun hath saved you three tymes the vallue of it, by accomplishing too good acts at once.’ The indigo now sent was bought before the receipt of the Company’s orders not to purchase any more of that commodity. The foregoing lines were prepared for dispatch by way of Bantam in the <em>Advice</em>, which was expected from Masulipatam by October 5; but on October 7 the <em>Antelope</em> touched here [on her way to Surat] and brought news that the <em>Advice</em> could not arrive before the end of the month (‘which is the daingerous time of all the yeare for fowle wether in this place’) and that it was even uncertain whether she would come at all. Winter and Thomas Alison (master of the <em>Advice</em>) are to blame for this. She was sent to Masulipatam on July 4 to mend a small defect in her rudder and then to bring back a quantity of rice, which would have made 100 per cent. profit; yet she was dispatched on August 10 without anything being done to the rudder, and the result was that before she had gone five leagues it dropped from her stern, and she was forced to return to Masulipatam. Her lading was then put on board the <em>Antelope</em>, which brought it here, as aforesaid; but now rice is much lower in price, owing to a quantity having recently arrived and to good rains having fallen. In view of the uncertainty of the arrival of the <em>Advice</em>, a quantity of cloth for Europe has been laden in the <em>Antelope</em>, which is now resuming her voyage for Surat. A consignment of saltpetre was placed on board the <em>Advice</em> before her departure; they can supply 100 tons annually. Forward their accounts, inventories, &amp;c. <em>PS.</em>—Cannot send the promised statement of their ‘quick stocke’, owing to the non-arrival of the Masulipatam accounts. Just as they were dispatching the <em>Antelope</em>, the <em>Advice</em> arrived. Hope to dispeed her to Bantam within three days. <em>Enclosure:</em> List of Europe goods sent in the <em>Antelope</em>, and of those brought by the <em>Advice</em> from Masulipatam. (<em>Copy, received at Bantam per the Advice November 26. 14½ pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-206"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="206" data-folio="168" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n205_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 168">[p. 168]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Gilbert Grimes, aboard the</span> <em>Greyhound</em> [<span class="sc">at Swally</span>], <span class="sc">to the President at Surat, October</span> 11, 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">A</span>, p. 264).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">His men are grumbling because they cannot get money to buy clothes. Adler has the means, if the President will sanction the payment. Is pushing on preparations for his voyage. (<em>Copy.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">John Adler at Swally Marine to the Same, October</span> 11, 1647 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 265).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>Seahorse</em> anchored this morning outside the bar. Grimes intends to beach his vessel on Wednesday, and therefore desires instructions as to the disposal of his broadcloth and coral. (<em>Copy.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Philip Wylde at Achin to</span> [<span class="sc">the President and Council at Surat</span>], <span class="sc">November</span> 5, 1647 (<em>O.C.</em> 2047).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">On May 27 the <em>Supply</em> arrived, bringing orders to him to make trial of the markets at ‘Jahore, Pearack, &amp;c.’ He thereupon applied to the Queen here for licence to trade at Perak, pointing out that the Dutch had a settled residence there; but ‘from Hir Majesty it was answered the Dutch had no such licence, and that, if we traded thither, the proffitt that hir subjects yearely received would be little.’ The desired permission was therefore absolutely refused, and on June 18 Wylde departed in the <em>Supply</em> for Johore. Malacca was reached on July 13. Wylde paid a visit to the Governor, who received him with much courtesy; but it was thought best to depart without notice on the 15th, fearing the Dutch might demand customs on the ship’s cargo. On July 27 the <em>Supply</em> anchored at the mouth of Johore River. The broker was sent up to the King, who ‘returned his boate and one of his people, with courteous expressions desireing me to bring the ship to his towne; but, unwilling to adventure hir thither till I was acquainted with the people, the 30th ditto I accompanied the messenger to His Majesty, presenting your letters with a present; which after perusuall I was dismissed. The next day following, he demanded my desires; when I answered it was your request to have free trade in all his parts and dominions, not paying customes; which he and his orankayes at first seemed to admire [i. e. wonder] my demands <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-207" data-page="207" data-folio="169" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n206_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 169">[p. 169]</button> were so large on so small accquaintance. His answere was for this yeare our ship off goods was custome free, but in the future not to be expected; and that I had licence to build warehouses and unlade my goods if I pleased. After certaine daies His Majestie sent messengers to make a contract how much money I would give him yearely upon a ship for trade; when I drew severall articles and promised him 100 taile [<em>tael</em>, the Chinese ounce of silver]; which after he had taken into consideracion, and consulted with his orankayes [<em>see the preceding volume</em>, p. 212] (who commands as much as himselfe), it was concluded to be dangerous to lett the English have any residence in his country, by reason they deny the priviledge to the Duch, which might cause warrs, to the dequiteing of his wonted quiet liveing. Therefore there answere was to me: whereas the King had promised custom free for this yeare, it was granted, but should not have any residence in his country longer then the ship stayed, and that should not trade to any part of his kingdome but in Jahore; and so, if you would condecend to paying the following duties and customes, you might have trade, vizt., to pay 5 per cent. off all goods brought thither for sale; to pay 6 masse Acheene on each bahar pepper bought; to pay 10 masse per bahar on tynn; to pay 104 tale lungargow<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-107" href="#sec-05-fn-107" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-107" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, or anchoridge, on each ship you send thither.’ His being thus crossed in his designs Wylde attributes to the skipper, &amp;c., of Hājī Zāhid Beg’s vessel, ‘in hopes to have all the trade to themselves; who paid not any custome the last yeare and have libberty to trade to all his ports. They were invited thither by the King from Mollacco, and therefore the more favoured.’ These Gujarātīs had sold all their goods and had bought up everything obtainable. Prices of the chief commodities, viz., tin, pepper, ‘aggula<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-108" href="#sec-05-fn-108" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-108" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>’, and elephants’ teeth. ‘Aggula’ is the principal product here. These goods come from ‘Phaung’, ‘Comper’, ‘Boolaung’, and ‘Keelaun’,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-109" href="#sec-05-fn-109" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-109" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> the first-named being ‘the greatest place of importance’. List of cotton, &amp;c., goods vendible in Johore. Wylde found the market glutted with Gujarāt cloth, and could sell no more than eight bales. Finding <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-208" data-page="208" data-folio="170" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n207_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 170">[p. 170]</button> no hope of a speedy improvement, and being anxious to get back to Achin in time to build a new warehouse there, he left Johore on August 28, after receiving from the King a reply to the President’s letter. The ship sailed from the river’s mouth on September 2, and on the 8th anchored near Malacca, owing to contrary winds. Two days later a Dutch ship was met, the captain of which demanded that the <em>Supply</em> should go back to Malacca; but this Wylde refused to do, unless the Dutch would undertake to make good any loss that might be caused to the Company by the resulting delay. To this they would not agree; so the voyage was resumed and the vessel reached Achin in safety on September 30. For what had happened there in his absence, Wylde refers to Reynardson’s letter. He hopes soon to give in person a fuller narrative of his experiences. (<em>Copy.</em> 3¼ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Thomas Reynardson</span> and <span class="sc">John Parmiter</span> at <span class="sc">Achin</span> to [<span class="sc">the President and Council at Surat</span>], <span class="sc">November</span> 5, 1647 (<em>O.C.</em> 2048).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>Supply</em> sent to Johore. On June 28 one of Courteen’s ships,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-110" href="#sec-05-fn-110" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-110" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> which was lying in this road, seized two Surat junks and threatened to carry them away unless a certain sum was paid. Thereupon the Company’s factors were imprisoned until they agreed to find the money. Later, upon complaint to the Queen, she ordered Courteen’s factor, William Cock, to repay the amount; and this has to a great extent been done. Among other things a fine diamond has been accepted at less than half its cost in England. ‘The 26th July here arrived three Dutch ships, which brought an embassador to this place, who demanded of the Queene the whole trade of Tecoo, Pryman, Andrapare, and Perack,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-111" href="#sec-05-fn-111" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-111" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> and that no Moores nor any other vessells should trade there besides the Queenes vessells; which she utterly denyed, and onely granted them 1,200 bahar of pepper yearely from the West Coast, custome free.’ Although last year Wylde was granted remission of customs, this year the factors have been repeatedly troubled with demands <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-209" data-page="209" data-folio="171" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n208_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 171">[p. 171]</button> of that nature. It is evidently useless to trust to promises, and the only way is to get every concession signed by the Queen, ‘as we have done for the cleareing ourselves, in case of any nations ceazeing any vessells belonging to this place.’ Markets here are so bad that scarcely any of the Company’s goods have been sold. Particulars of the return cargo. It is credibly reported that the Dutch have stopped the Perak fleet; ‘if true, this place is quite spoyled’, for there will then be no hope of either money or goods. One of their house servants has poisoned himself; he had been detected in stealing goods and feared punishment. Little or nothing should be sent here next year. Disposal of freight goods brought by the <em>Supply</em>. Reasons for not sending any in her now. At Wylde’s request, they certify that he has cleared his accounts in this place. He intends to go home at once, and Reynardson desires leave to follow him next year. Have put on board some rattans, the owner of which has not been traced. Goods sent belonging to the estates of Downs and Scattergood. Money advanced to Wylde for sea provisions. (<em>Copy. 2¼ pp.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1">[<span class="sc">John Spiller at Tatta</span>] <span class="sc">to the Same</span>, [<span class="sc">November —</span>] 1647 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">A</span>, p. 268).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Answers theirs of September 13, received on October 25. His arrival from Kandiāro was advised in his letter of September 30. Has only been able to procure 330 corge of cloth from that place: Thanks them for their encouragement to continue serving the Company here. Cannot get any offers for the lead. Will do his best to obtain from the saltpetre men what they owe. ‘These Tahtah merchants were about a moneth since very hott in buying cloath both in Nusserpore and Khodearah; but not as yet any shipp arriving from Conggo, Bossorah, or any other parts hath well quelled them. But that that most affrights them is report of a great French shipp seeing (<em>sic</em>) lying about Muscatt, which, report sayes, tooke a small vessell lading (<em>sic</em>) with coho [coffee] and bound for Bossora. The coho they took out of her and then lett her and her men goe. This prementioned shipp is said to be a very great one and to have 80 gunns and 3 or 400 men in her. That the <em>Seahorse</em> is not yet arrived is much admired by all, and I hartily wishe her well and that she may arrive in safetye; as doe all these merchants, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-210" data-page="210" data-folio="172" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n209_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 172">[p. 172]</button> for by her they expect most of theire mony. Heare hath for these 4 or 5 dayes been a great noise of two shipps seen before this barr, riding in 20 fadoms of water and sometimes nearer, and their gunns heard to goe of very often; but nobody knowes what they are, nor dare the pilotts goe aboard. I have sent a man to Bundar, yf possible to learne what they are; and when I know the truth you shall be made acquainted therwith.’ His Nasarpur cloth has been packed and sent down to the port; and he is now doing the same with that from Kandiāro. [<em>The rest is missing.</em>] (<em>Copy.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">SAMUEL WILTON AT GOMBROON TO [THE PRESIDENT AND COUNCIL AT SURAT], DECEMBER 9, 1647 (<em>O.C.</em> 2051).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Answers their letter of November 5, received by the Dutch <em>Juffrouw</em>. Heynes died three days ago. Encloses a list of prices and ‘the just length of a covitt shaw<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-112" href="#sec-05-fn-112" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-112" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’. Explains why certain coins were recently sent. Agrees that the broker here is untrustworthy. Notes the orders for the punctual dispatch of ships and for hiring the factory house by the year only; a grant of it might have been obtained when the last present was given to the King, but now the matter must be put off till next year. Cannot at present answer about the freighting of one of the Company’s vessels to Jiddah by a native merchant, as the latter is still at Lar. Will try to procure samples of silk from merchants, but it will be necessary to give a bribe to the Wazīr of Ispahān. Lewis wrote on November [   ] that he would be leaving that city in a few days, having stayed some time to collect a debt. Mahmūd Taqī, who has been appointed Shāhbandar here, is father-in-law of the son of the Sultān of Ispahān; so there is likely to be ‘a great affinitie betweene a couple of knaves’. If the former is the man who was Shāhbandar four or five years ago, ‘an archer theefe cannot be found in this Kings dominions. This shotter<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-113" href="#sec-05-fn-113" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-113" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> departed Spahan the 19th ultimo; and six daies after the King was to goe a hunting with the Indian embassador. His unparralleld nett, carryed by 60 or 70 camells, was sent out, and 36,000 men appointed for the manageing that and surrounding the mountaines. From thence the Indian embassador sett forward for India, not retourning back for Spahan. The <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-211" data-page="211" data-folio="173" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n210_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 173">[p. 173]</button> Pollonia [i. e. Polish] embassador likewise is gone, having received a pisca[s]h of 35 tomands and one bale [of] silke, and as much uppon his retinew. While hee is in the country, he is to be furnished with 60 horses or mules and 30 abacees per day.’ Requests a supply of rice, butter, lamp oil, and candles, as these articles are now very dear. For butter they used to pay from 6 to 8 shāhīs per Tabrīz maund, but now it costs 11 or 12; while the price of candles has risen from 17 or 18 to 26 shāhīs. <em>PS.</em>— Heynes left no will. (<em>Copy.</em> 3 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">[<span class="sc">The President and Council at Surat to the President and Council at Bantam, December</span> —, 1647] (<em>O.C.</em> 1963).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-114" href="#sec-05-fn-114" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-114" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">. . . Will supply the piece-goods desired. As the Company’s business now stands, there is little encouragement to revive the Manilla trade. Have rectified a mistake in their books regarding the last consignment of calicoes. Deficiency in the pepper received from Bantam by the <em>Hart</em>. Send some rosewater, and promise a further quantity. ‘Wee take notice in your severall letters of your extraordinary want of meanes and shipping; the former of which putts us in minde of our owne sadd condicion. But of shipping we have too many, considering the times at present, which are soe badd that we can neither finde employment for them upon fraighting voyages nor dare lade them for the Company; both Persia, Bussora, and Mocho being so overcloyed that the last yeares goods were sold to loss. But that which most prejudiceth our fraughts, both the last and this year, is the number of great Dutch shipps which attend that employment, whereof here are noe less then six in Swally Hole at present, which soe farr exceede our small pinnaces, both for security and accommodacion that’ [<em>part missing</em>] . . . Padre Alexander Rodes<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-115" href="#sec-05-fn-115" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-115" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> arrived on the <em>Hind</em> in <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-212" data-page="212" data-folio="174" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n211_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 174">[p. 174]</button> safety; but the other Portuguese passenger, Francisco de Cirpa<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-116" href="#sec-05-fn-116" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-116" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, died in the voyage, and his goods have been forwarded to the Padres at Damān, for transmission to Goa. Private trade found in the <em>Hind</em>. The goods now sent in her are for the account of the Fourth Joint Stock. Arrival of the <em>Eagle</em> and <em>Blessing</em>, which left the Downs on April 28. The <em>Mary</em> [bound for Bantam] accompanied them as far as Cape Verd. The cargoes they brought amounted to 52,674<em>l.</em> 8<em>s.</em> 4<em>d.</em> On the 2nd ultimo the pinnace <em>Lanneret</em> arrived from Basra and Gombroon, ‘with the accustomed complaints of bad marketts.’ She brought from Basra, in money and goods, 30,446 rials of eight, and from Gombroon 449,403 shāhīs. On October 10 the <em>Blessing</em> was dispatched to Rājāpur, Goa, and Bhatkal, with orders to buy at the last-named place pepper, or, failing that, arrack, coir, and cowries. The 12th ultimo the <em>Lanneret</em> sailed for Rājāpur with money and goods to pay what is owing at Rāybāg. She returned with some pepper on the 1st current, and was followed two days later by the <em>Blessing</em>. On the 4th the <em>Falcon</em> arrived from Cape Comorin and Cochin. Goods received from Agra, &amp;c. . . . (4 <em>pp. Damaged.</em>)</p></div><h2 class="sec-h1"><span class="sc">Richard Hudson’s Account of Events at Balasore</span>, <span class="sc">December</span> 26, 1647 (<em>O.C.</em> 2056).</h2><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">On their first arrival they were well received; but a change was soon brought about by the appearance of a Danish fleet of five vessels, which seized a Moorish ship containing eight elephants. At the entreaty of Malik Beg [the Governor?], the English went twice to the Danes to intercede for the release of the junk, but could not prevail; whereupon the Moors told them that, as the Danes and English were alike Christians, any damage done by the former would have to be satisfied by the latter. Thus matters remained until October 10. In the interim arrived a letter from the Nawāb, brought by a captain and 500 horse. The English were required to meet the missive four or five miles out of town, but refused to do so, on the ground that this was a new custom; and thereupon it was brought to their house. It proved to contain ‘an imperious command for our stay’. The factors refused to obey, and their <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-213" data-page="213" data-folio="175" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n212_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 175">[p. 175]</button> vessel ‘fell a litle lower’, as if to depart. On this being perceived, ‘some 1,000 souldiers were called from the adjacent places, and suddainly they made a mudd wall and planted nine great gunns (in interim of our talkeing); and that night they made three shott at us and spoild some of our fore riggins. The next day they plant[ed] other two gunns to play on our bowe, and other two in the Nabobs jounck in the dock, and other three pieces by the Princes ship, all which in less then 300 foote of our ship. Perceiveing this, and that there was noe hopes to gett downe (nor could without the helpe of boates, which was denied), we resolved to stay longer, and would have carryed our ship to a more convenient birth (because there was noe probability of getting downe, for they had stackt [i. e. staked] the river in six or seven places and sunck three or four boates, and six or eight mallangees <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-117" href="#sec-05-fn-117" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-117" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> boates filled with wood, straw, and such combustible stuff, to fire us), if it had been possible for us to have gotten past their fortification; but were prevented by 10 or 14 shott from the shoare and shipps in the dock, with 200 small shott and many arrowes. The tyde being spent and the wind blowing fresh at N. E., we were compeld to fall downe to the first birth, where we roade till one or two of the clock without shooteing; when it seemes [be]cause of our sufferrences they esteemd us their owne, and like a flock of tygers with open mouthes they came upon us, fireing the <em>Freindships</em> bancksall [i. e. warehouse] and heaveing dust at us. At last our patience could hold no longer. We addrest our selves to our gunns, and for three or four howres we made warme worke; and truly I conceive each was glad to be at quiett. The Dutch have escaped noe better, for their busines was altogeather stopt till the 6th [?] or 7th December, when they also enjoyed licence, or rather forct it by landing 60 men and ten pieces [of] ordinance, which they mounted on their howse and banksall <sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-118" href="#sec-05-fn-118" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-118" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, keepeing one of their three ships they had in Piplee alwaies to scower the river, goeing downe with the ebb and up with the flood.’ This attack was entirely unprovoked, ‘as under their hands I can shew.’ (<em>Copy</em>. 2 <em>pp</em>.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-214"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="214" data-folio="176" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n213_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 176">[p. 176]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">President Breton at Surat to [the Company], December</span> [         ], 1647 (<em>O. C.</em> 2057).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Renews the appeal he made last year for permission to return to England, and also craves their favour in the following matter. The Persian silk ordered in their latest letter cannot be provided in time for the present fleet; and therefore he ventures to send for sale on his own account ten loads which he bought several months ago through an Armenian merchant. He would not have meddled with this commodity but for the fact that the Company had not desired any for a long time; and he conceives that he is doing some service by enabling them to try the market by this means. The said silk forms ‘the whole import and entyre hopes of my future subsistance’, and he begs that it may be sold on his account and the proceeds delivered to his brother, William Breton, ‘minister att Clapton in Northamptonshire’.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-119" href="#sec-05-fn-119" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-119" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> (1½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">List of the Company’s Servants in the Surat Presidency</span> (<em>O.C.</em> 2070).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-05-fn-120" href="#sec-05-fn-120" data-fn-target="sec-05-fn-120" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>At Surat</em>: Francis Breton (President), 350<em>l.</em>; Thomas Merry, 300<em>l.</em>; Andrew Baines, minister, 50<em>l.</em>; Richard Fitch, 50<em>l.</em>; Edward Pearce, 60<em>l.</em>; Thomas Breton, 25<em>l.</em>; Anthony Clitherow, 60<em>l.</em>; Thomas Andrews, 30<em>l.</em>; John Goodyear, 20<em>l.</em>; John Parmiter, 20<em>l.</em>; Henry Young, 18<em>l.</em>; Walter Gollofer, 18<em>l.</em>; John Adler, 18<em>l.</em>; and Thomas Cook, 35<em>l. Also</em> Daniel Elder, ‘intended for Suratt’, 70<em>l. At Agra</em>: Edward Knipe, 200<em>l.</em>; Francis Hammersly, 80<em>l.</em>; Richard Davidge, 110<em>l.</em>; William Jesson, 80<em>l.</em>; John Burnell, 30<em>l.</em>; Joshua Blackwell, 20<em>l. At Ahmadābād</em>: George Tash, 100<em>l.</em>; Hugh Fenn, 110<em>l.</em>; Anthony Smith, 60<em>l.</em>; Isaiah Joyce, 12<em>l. In ‘Synda’</em>: John Spiller, 133<em>l.</em> 6<em>s.</em> 8<em>d.</em>; Henry Garry, 80<em>l.</em>; Nicholas Scrivener, 30<em>l.</em>; Gilbert Harrison, 20<em>l. In Persia</em>: Robert Heynes, 25<em>l.</em>; John Lewis, 30<em>l.</em>; Samuel Wilton, 20<em>l.</em>; Thomas Best, 24<em>l.</em> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-215" data-page="215" data-folio="177" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories08fost/page/n214_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 177">[p. 177]</button> <em>At Mokha:</em> George Oxenden, 35<em>l.</em>; Joseph Cross, 18<em>l.</em>; Nicholas Buckeridge, 6<em>l.</em>; Charles Milward, 15<em>l.</em>; John Broadbent, 6<em>l.</em>; Lawrence Cheny, 6<em>l. At Achin:</em> Philip Wylde, 70<em>l.</em>; Thomas Reynardson, 60<em>l.</em>; Francis Scattergood, 15<em>l.</em>; John Rose, 12<em>l. At Basra:</em> Robert Cranmer, 30<em>l.</em>; Revett Walwyn, 18<em>l.</em>; Thomas Cogan, 20<em>l.</em>; William Weale, 20<em>l. Total of salaries:</em> 2,619<em>l.</em> 6<em>s.</em> 8<em>d.</em> (3 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><section class="apparatus-zone" aria-label="Footnotes"><h2 class="apparatus-heading">Critical Apparatus &amp; Notes</h2><ol class="apparatus-list"><li id="sec-05-fn-001" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-001" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The survivors of Smart’s colony.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-002" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-002" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Rebecca</em>, which had been dispatched from Achin on August 25, 1646 (see Brit. Mus. <em>Addl. MS.</em> 14,037, ff. 33, 40, &amp;c.).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-003" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-003" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably ‘dilations’ (i. e. procrastinations) was intended.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-004" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-004" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Al-Nawāb.</em></span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-005" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-005" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A copyist’s error for ‘painters’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-006" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-006" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Another copy will be found at p. 51 of <em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">A</span>. The original letter was received on January 30.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-007" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-007" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Shehr or Shuhair, on the southern coast of Arabia.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-008" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-008" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Arabic <em>mausim</em>, ‘season,’ particularly the pilgrim season, which was also the chief period of business activity. This word is the origin of the familiar ‘monsoon’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-009" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-009" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Antelope</em> (see p. 96).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-010" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-010" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A copy of this postscript forms <em>O.C.</em> 2020.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-011" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-011" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is another copy in <em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii <span class="sc">a</span> (p. 88). This gives the date of receipt as March 12.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-012" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-012" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The other copy has ‘1000’, but this is probably an error.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-013" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-013" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Another copy will be found at p. 59 of vol. cii <span class="sc">A</span> of <em>Factory Records, Surat</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-014" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-014" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>O.C.</em> 2014 and 2018.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-015" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-015" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is a copy in the <em>O.C. Duplicates</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-016" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-016" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>O.C.</em> 2026 says ’10th’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-017" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-017" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A play upon the name of the Company’s Warehouse Keeper (John Blount).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-018" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-018" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">At five mahmūdīs to the rial of eight this would mean a profit of nearly 180 per cent.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-019" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-019" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This piece of ground was afterwards ‘lost by the sea gaining upon itt’ (Wilson’s <em>Early Annals</em>, vol. ii. part ii. p. 269).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-020" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-020" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Sar-Khail</em> or chief revenue official at Golconda (see the 1634–36 volume, p. 325).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-021" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-021" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘But he is dead on the waie home’ (<em>marginal note</em>)</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-022" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-022" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>See Court Minutes, 1644–49</em>, p. 134.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-023" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-023" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">From a marginal note it appears that payment was to be made at the rate of 5<em>s.</em> per rial of eight.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-024" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-024" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Court Minutes 1644–49</em>, p. 139.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-025" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-025" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For a list of the packet sent by the <em>Dolphin</em> see <em>O.C.</em> 2025. A copy is among the <em>O.C. Duplicates</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-026" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-026" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Described later as Mr. Merry’s servant. He was a distant relative of the Diarist (see Wheatley’s <em>Pepysiana</em>, p. 10), and appears to have died at Surat in 1649 or 1650.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-027" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-027" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably the yard on the river bank below Surat, where the Company’s small vessels were repaired.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-028" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-028" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Master of the <em>Antelope</em>. He appears to have been the son of John Proud, who was in command of the <em>Dolphin</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-029" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-029" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">For consultations held on board, rom June to October, see <em>Marine Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. iv. no. 20. There also may be found copies of two letters left at St. Augustine’s Bay, and one left at Mayotta.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-030" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-030" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">See a note on p. 182 of the 1642–45 volume.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-031" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-031" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly <em>Daryā-daulat</em>, ‘the river of wealth’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-032" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-032" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sanganer, seven miles south of Jaipur. The town was famous for its dyed and stamped chintzes.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-033" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-033" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Smart had sent the <em>Friendship</em> from Achin to Malacca on October 8, 1646, to sell some wine she had procured at Madeira on her way out; but she returned in January without having sold her cargo (<em>Brit. Mus. Addl. MS.</em> 14,037, ff. 40, 42, 47).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-034" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-034" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This letter was carried to Surat by the Dutch <em>Leeuwerik</em>. There is another copy in <em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. (p. 65).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-035" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-035" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The ‘catchaes’ of p. 13; see also p. 106 <em>infra</em>, and the previous volume, p. 252.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-036" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-036" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Including some ‘dustmalls [<em>dastmāl</em>] or romall [<em>rūmāl</em>] dusters [<em>dastār</em>]’. All three words mean a napkin or towel.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-037" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-037" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The two ambassadors were Nicolaas Verburgh or Vanderburgh and Willem Bastinck (who has been already mentioned at p. 271 of the previous volume). See the introduction (p. xvii) to Hotz’s <em>Journaal der Reis van .... Joan Cunaeus</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-038" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-038" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A copyist’s error for ‘Noorooze’, i. e. <em>Naurōz</em>, the festival of the vernal equinox.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-039" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-039" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">This date is given in a later letter (April 17).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-040" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-040" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Bairam</em> or <em>bairamī</em> was the name of a very fine cotton stuff.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-041" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-041" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pers. <em>sāhibī</em>, a kind of silk cloth.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-042" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-042" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Satin (Ar. <em>atlas</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-043" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-043" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Not identified. Probably the copyist’s perversion of the original.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-044" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-044" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This seems to be used here in the sense of ‘disrecourse’, and may indeed be a copyist’s error for that word.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-045" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-045" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently identical with ‘Chout Tocker’ [Chhotā Thākur?], described later as the Company’s chief broker at Surat.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-046" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-046" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Received in London from Courteen’s ship, the <em>Sun</em>, on January 14, 1648, together with other papers listed in <em>O.C.</em> 2030 A copy of this letter, apparently sent overland and received December 6, 1647, forms <em>O.C.</em> 2029.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-047" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-047" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Shawls (Mahr. <em>pāmarī</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-048" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-048" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the 1634–36 volume, p. 219.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-049" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-049" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably an abbreviation for <em>nīm</em> (half)-<em>guldār</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-050" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-050" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">On this cloth, which seems to be the same as the ‘sallooes’ of p. 79, see <em>Hobson-Jobson</em>, s. v. ‘Shalee’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-051" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-051" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Thomas Andrews, one of the leaders of the Parliamentarian party in the City. He was elected an Alderman in 1642, Sheriff the same year, and Lord Mayor in 1649 and 1650; and was knighted by Cromwell in November, 1657 (information from the Rev. A. B. Beaven). He became a Committee of the East India Company in 1643, Deputy-Governor in 1657, and Governor two years later, but died soon after taking office.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-052" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-052" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Kornelis Vanderlyn had acted as Governor-General at Batavia since the death of Van Diemen in April, 1645, but the news of his confirmation in that post by the Dutch Company did not reach Batavia until October, 1646.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-053" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-053" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The bore in the Mahī estuary has always been a great hindrance to navigation.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-054" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-054" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">An abstract of her cargo will be found in <em>Factory Records, Persia</em>, vol. i.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-055" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-055" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A marginal note gives the date as February 13, but this seems to be ten days too late (cf. p. 100).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-056" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-056" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">As the factors came up by water, a <em>ghāt</em>, or landing-place, is probably intended. In that case the ‘Tugghen’ of p. 134 may have been the spot.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-057" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-057" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Bāzār, or market.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-058" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-058" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Murād Bakhsh, fourth son of the Emperor.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-059" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-059" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Bukkur, a fortress on an island in the Indus, between Sukkur and Rohri.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-060" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-060" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Gambat, twelve miles south of Khairpur. It was once a centre of the cotton-weaving industry.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-061" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-061" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mr. G. P. Tate explains this as <em>Chahil-yak</em>, or one-fortieth, i. e. 2½ per cent.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-062" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-062" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>arbāb</em>, a lord or master.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-063" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-063" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mr. Tate informs me that this word in Sindi means ‘a kick’. The sense here is evidently ‘a fine’, imposed by way of punishment.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-064" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-064" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Perhaps ‘choultries’ is intended.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-065" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-065" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Order for payment (<em>barāt</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-066" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-066" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Likewise called Mahābat Khān. He was afterwards twice Governor of Kābul, and enjoyed the favour of both Shāh Jahān and Aurangzeb.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-067" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-067" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Dāroghā</em> here means a chief of customs</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-068" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-068" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Added by Spiller at Darbēlo. The date is indecipherable.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-069" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-069" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Enclosed in the foregoing.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-070" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-070" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>sarkār</em>: see note on p. 160 of the 1618-21 volume.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-071" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-071" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note on p. 300 of the previous volume.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-072" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-072" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘Rim’ or ‘rime’ is an old English word for a skin or membrane.
