Possibly the reference is to the Indian way of packing this particular kind of indigo.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-073" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-073" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Cakes or balls: see p. 31 <em>n.</em> The word seems to be connected with Latin <em>guttae</em>, ‘drops’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-074" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-074" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note on p. 110. The cloth was of a Turkey-red colour, and therefore suitable for the purpose indicated.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-075" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-075" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The portion containing the date has disappeared; but the reference to the departure of the <em>Antelope</em> for Masulipatam (April 25) furnishes the necessary clue. Apparently this letter was sent by an Indian junk.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-076" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-076" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See p. 101.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-077" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-077" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Arabic <em>ashrafī</em>, applied to various gold coins.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-078" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-078" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Qūrchī Bāshī</em> (see note on p. 297 of the preceding volume).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-079" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-079" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Meshed: Herāt, the old name of which was Heri (cf. the Heri Rud): and Farrah, a town now mostly in ruins, but once a place of importance as the frontier town of Persia on the caravan route between Ispahān and Kandahār.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-080" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-080" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This does not agree with the statement on p. 124.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-081" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-081" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The temporary encampment on the beach at Swally.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-082" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-082" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Aguada</em>, or watering-place, near Goa: see a note on p. 7 of the 1637–41 volume.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-083" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-083" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly <em>Bohrās</em>, who in Gujarāt are often peasant-cultivators.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-084" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-084" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sāmlod is about 11 miles north-east of Broach. A <em>thāna</em> was a military or police post.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-085" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-085" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Antelope</em> and <em>Greyhound</em>. Thomas Gee was master of the <em>William</em>, and John Proud of the <em>Dolphin</em>. They commenced their voyage in April, 1646 (see p. 76).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-086" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-086" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The master, purser, and chief mate respectively.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-087" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-087" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly the present village of Tuggur, about 5 miles west of Darbēlo. It is not now on the river.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-088" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-088" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently <em>qāsid</em> (see p. 100) is intended.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-089" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-089" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Congee (Fr. <em>congé</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-090" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-090" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For an earlier mention of this man see the 1637–41 volume, p. 269.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-091" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-091" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">In his journal of the outward voyage of the <em>Bonito</em>, given on a later page, Wylde makes it clear that the reference here is to the Bay of Trincomali, of which he gives a drawing and the following description: ‘In my voyage to Bantam in the <em>Seaflower</em> we anchored ther, and in a very fine bay, all sandy ground. Wee staied there nine dayes, in which time wee bought henns, limes, and such fruite as the country did affoard; three henns for a fanan Madras (which is three pence), one hundredweight limes for a fanan, and large dryed fish very plentifull and very cheape. Very good hunting of large staggs, wild hoggs, and very good fouling; peacockes, duck, teale, brangeese [i.e. brentgeese], and large hearnes are very plentifull. Our water wee filled out of the wells which were digged by the Portugall[s], but since there departure many stoped up. Here is a very large lake or pond of fresh water as you goe towards the black pointe, where we did swim and wash ourselves, which I thinke is very dry.... Here may ride a 1000 saile of shipps and never be discovered.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-092" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-092" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is a Beilul Bay opposite Mokha, with a village some distance inland.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-093" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-093" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The postscript, which is in a fragmentary state, evidently recounts the intelligence, received overland, that the Scots had surrendered King Charles to the Parliament.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-094" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-094" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably the Jacob Cottenshaw of the 1637–41 volume.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-095" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-095" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Marmagao, on the southern side of Goa harbour.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-096" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-096" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently one of the long-vanished branches of the Indus.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-097" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-097" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Kusumbha</em>, or safflower, the flowers of which yield a red dye.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-098" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-098" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>ramazān</em>, the Muhammadan month of fasting.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-099" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-099" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sirohi, in Rajputana, 20 miles north-east of Abu. Merta is in Jodhpur State, about 65 miles north-east of the capital.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-100" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-100" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Finch (<em>Purchas</em>, vol. i. p. 430), describing the manufacture of indigo at Biāna in 1610, says that it was ‘spread on cloth, to dry in the sunne; and beeing a little hardened, they take it in their hands and, making small balls, lay them on the sand to dry (for any other thing would drinke up the colour): this is the cause of the sandy foot.’</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-101" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-101" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mehmadābād, 17 miles south of Ahmadābād.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-102" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-102" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Malay <em>gebar</em>, commonly applied to a sheet of coarse white cloth, but also used as an adjective to imply an extra large size of other kinds of piece-goods. Saris (<em>Purchas</em>, vol. i, p. 391) says ‘a Goober is double and containeth twelve yards’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-103" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-103" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pātan, in Baroda State, about 60 miles north-west of Ahmadābād. According to the <em>Imperial Gazetteer</em> it still manufactures ‘<em>patolas</em> (variegated sārīs)’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-104" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-104" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mirjān, in North Kanara, about 20 miles north of Honāwar. See the account of it given by Fryer (Crooke’s edition, vol. i. p. 150).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-105" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-105" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Christ-cross was the pious sign placed at the beginning of the alphabet in the old horn-books; while the Ampersand (i. e. the character &amp;) came at the end. The phrase is equivalent, therefore, to our modern ‘from A to Z’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-106" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-106" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the previous volume, p. 38.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-107" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-107" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Langar</em> is Persian for an anchor.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-108" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-108" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Port. <em>aguila</em>, eagle-wood, also called lignum-aloes. It was used for incense.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-109" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-109" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pahang, Kampar (Sumatra), Bulang (an island south of Singapore), and Kelang (Salangor).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-110" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-110" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Friendship</em>. For the correspondence on this subject between the two sets of factors see <em>O.C.</em> 2038–2044.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-111" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-111" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Tiku, Priaman, Indrapura, and Perak.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-112" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-112" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Shāhī</em>, royal. Fryer says that ‘the King’s cobit consists of inches 36’.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-113" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-113" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently a messenger mounted on a camel (Pers. <em>shutur</em>) is meant.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-114" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-114" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This consists only of two fragments of the letter. The date is inferred from the contents.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-115" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-115" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This was the celebrated Jesuit Father Alexandre de Rhodes, who was now returning to Europe after twenty-five years’ labour in the East. In his narrative of his experiences (1653) he relates his voyage from Macassar in an English ship to Bantam; his courteous reception there by President Baker; his departure for Surat, where he found an equally warm welcome from Breton; and his subsequent voyage, early in 1648, in an English ship to Gombroon, whence he proceeded overland by way of Ispahān and Tabrīz to Smyrna, and so to Italy.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-116" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-116" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Rhodes calls him François Serpa, and mentions his death during the voyage.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-117" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-117" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>malanjī</em>, a salt-maker.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-118" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-118" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1647-48, p. 38.</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-119" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-119" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Company permitted the silk to be sold, but declined to pay over to Breton’s brother more than half the proceeds, reserving the rest until investigations had been made into the President’s private trade (<em>Court Minutes</em>, 1644–49, pp. 282, 297, &amp;c.).</span></li><li id="sec-05-fn-120" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-05-fn-120" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Endorsed as ‘presented in Court’, March 8, 1648. The list was therefore prepared at home, and this accounts for the appearance in it of several factors who were in fact dead by that date. The Committees were at the time discussing what reductions were possible, both in factories and factors (<em>Court Minutes</em>, 1644–49, p. 261).</span></li></ol></section>
